| MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 
I 


CETTINJE 
I 

It was in the autumn of 1912 that, in company with 
a friend in the army who had wangled a few weeks’ 
leave, I first set eyes on the Dalmatian coast, and 
it was without premeditation, acting on a sudden 
impulse, that at Gravosa I decided to follow Basil 
(who had gone on to Cattaro) and spend the last 
few days of my holiday in a visit to Montenegro. 

The boat —it was the Baron Gautch, at that 
time the finest of the Lloyd steamers — left Gravosa 
at eight on a lovely October morning and with re- 
gret I said good-bye to the long narrow bay, closed 
in by wooded hills dotted with lovely villas and 
palazzt. We swung clear of the quay, passed the 
black 8. Y. Vectis, which had anchored the day be- 
fore, rounded Punta Lapad, and steering south 
through the Bocca Grande, steamed along the 
wooded coast till Ragusa, jutting out into the sea, 
surrounded and half-hidden by its walls and towers, 
burst upon us in all its medizval splendour. After 
leaving the island of Lacroma on our left, we passed 
the bay of Breno and the rock-islands of Bobara 
and Merkan and came abreast of the dilapidated 
but once famous town of Ragusa Vecchia. The 
coast after this became arid, monotonous, rocky, 
till at last, rounding the sharp Punta d’Ostro, we 
sailed by the forts on the rock of Rondoni and on 


184 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


the Punta d’Arza into the entrance of the celebrated 
Bocche de Cattaro. 

The excitement on board now became intense. 
Every one got out his guide-book and his spy- 
glasses, and took up the best position available and 
spoke in whispers. I was told over and over again, 
in guttural English or in German-French, that there 
wasn’t anything like it in the world. My heart sank 
lower and lower, expecially when I discovered that 
Baekeder likened it to Lake Lucerne. Scenic splen- 
dours of an obvious kind have always left me a 
little cold, ever since a disastrous journey in my 
youth to that very lake. Then I duly ascended the 
Righi and Mount Pilatus, and went to Goshenen 
and Andermatt. I have pictured the kind of 
mountains that are raved about, since that time, as 
visited by schoolmasters and ‘really keen men’ (in 
the undergraduate sense) and peopled by fodelling 
milkmen with a tendency to goitre. And I date 
from that holiday my preference for the little pri- 
vate English mountains on whose bosoms I can hide 
myself and be alone — hills that I can climb com- 
fortably in an afternoon, unencumbered by ugly 
boots and a stick with a spike in it. 

Long before we got to Castelnuovo, the first 
lovely port in the Bocche, all these prejudices had 
been swept away. There was something barbaric 
and untamable about the aspect of the great fjord 
which contrasted delightfully with the traces, on 
every side, of a most ancient civilization and cul- 
ture. I had read very little about the history of the 
district, but almost at once I had an intuition that 
great things had happened here. 

The Bocche consist of three large harbours joined 


CETTINJE - 185 


to one another by narrow canals, and walled in on 
almost every side by high and sinister mountains. 
Castelnuovo was an attractive spot. The town 
nestled in a mass of greenery under the shadow of 
Fort Spagnuolo, while farther back rose a cloud- 
draped hilltop. From the steamer one could see its 
ancient ivy-clad walls, contrasting oddly with its 
brand-new Hétel Boka. The situation of Castel- 
nuovo was said to be the most agreeable for resi- 
dence of any of the towns of the Bocche, while the 
place had some importance, even in 1912, as the 
terminus of a railway leading to Gravosa, and 
thence inland to Sarajevo and Buda-Pesth. The 
little molo was too small for the big steamer to be 
moored against it, so she anchored in the bay and 
the passengers were landed in boats. 

After Castelnuovo the grandeur of the scenery 
increased. We passed through the narrow Canale 
di Kumbur and entered the great triangular-shaped 
Baja di Teodo, largest of the three harbours, where 
a whole navy could lie concealed. The shores of the 
bay were green and thickly wooded and dotted with 
pretty houses. Many of the officers in the Austrian 
Navy were said to come and live on the Bocche 
when they retired, and the Bocchese have for 
centuries been celebrated seafaring men. 

Sailing straight across the baja, we came to the 
narrowest canal, less than half a mile across, called 
Le Catene, because it used to be protected by a 
chain boom. And once through this channel, we 
found ourselves in the innermost and most mag- 
nificent of the three loughs. Straight in front of us 
under the towering height of Monte Casson, and 
looking as though it had barely enough room to 


186 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


stand on, was the once important city of Perasto, 
with its decrepit campanile. It stands on a spit of 
land separating the Bay of Risano on the left from 
the Golfo di Cattaro on the right, and was then 
little more than a village, full of decayed, half- 
ruinous villas. What it was that had caused this 
distress I couldn’t discover. The town of Risano, 
they told me, had sunk in population from four 
thousand in 1850 to thirteen hundred, and Perasto 
had suffered even more. Apparently there used to 
be a considerable shipbuilding industry at one time 
at these two places, which has now disappeared. I 
longed to explore Perasto, but never got the chance. 
One of the officers of the ship pointed out to me the 
two little islands in front of it of San Giorgio and La 
Madonna. On the latter was a church containing, 
he said, a very holy picture, painted by Saint Luke. 
It was brought to Perasto in the fifteenth century, 
but moved of its own accord, surrounded by lighted 
candles, to this island of La Madonna. When it had 
done this three times it was allowed to remain. The 
great festival of the Madonna della Scalpello, as it 
is called, is on the 12th of July. There used, also, to 
be another public holiday on the day of the Assump- 
tion, August 15th, when a national ball was held, 
celebrated for the variety and gorgeousness of the 
costumes. My informant didn’t seem to know if 
these festivals were still kept alive in the beautiful, 
deserted town. From Risano to Perasto, and on 
through the straggling village of Dobrota to Cat- 
taro, a road runs along the eastern shore which must 
be one of the loveliest in Europe. 

With each turn of the screw the scene now be- 
came more and more awiul and silencing. The great 


CETTINJE 187 


limestone mountains towered up into the clouds all 
along the eastern side, exquisite in colouring — 
grey, dark blue, mauve. At their feet, balancing on 
a narrow strip of land between the crags and the 
shore, were white villas bowered in trees, while 
every now and then villages reflected themselves in 
the still, dark-blue waters. The extreme fertility of 
the outer shores of the Bocche lessens as one pro- 
ceeds inland and its appearance becomes more and 
more that of a fjord. At the very end, at Cattaro, 
the wall of mountains is so high that sunset falls 
there hours earlier than anywhere else on the 
Adriatic, and in winter it grows dark soon after two 
o'clock. 

I had gone on to the lower deck right up in the 
bows, during the latter part of the journey, to get a 
better view, and stayed there until we were along- 
side the molo. The broad quay was crowded with 
people to watch our arrival, and the whole scene 
was delicious. In front was the little Venetian city, 
dominated by its series of fortifications running up 
the side of a rocky hill and connected with one an- 
other by stone walls; and higher, at the back, rose 
the huge, frowning mountains, which culminated in 
the peak of Lovcen, which is the legendary monte 
negro. 

Cattaro looked very gay in the midday sun, jut- 
ting out on its little tongue of fertile land into the 
transparent, still water. In the crowd that met us 
were girls, dressed fashionably in the modes of Paris 
or Vienna, smart-looking officers, Montenegrins, 
vain as peacocks in their elaborate and costly na- 
tional dress, and hordes of unshorn, unwashed, 
villainous-looking facchini. The lovely gardens just 


188 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


by the quay-side looked delightful, the promenade 
outside the town walls —the Marina — against 
which rows of feluccas and trabaccolt were moored, 
a delicious place for idling. I knew Basil would 
not have moved very far from here, and that 
I should infallibly discover him in a café, drinking 
his apérizf. 


II 


Basil, of course, was sitting outside the café 
which faces the Porta Marina, by which you must 
enter the piazza from the quay. His handsome, 
bronzed face and dark, military moustache were 
creating their usual effect, while the Austrian ofh- 
cers were eyeing his perfectly cut breeches with 
ill-concealed envy. He looked half-asleep, had an 
unfinished drink in front of him and, of course, 
it hadn’t occurred to him to go anywhere. He 
watched my harassing struggle with the porters 
with immense appreciation, and without offering 
to help! 

They were terrible fellows, these porters. Nearly 
all spoke a little broken English. One of them, 
Mato Golub by name, held out to me an elegantly 
printed visiting-card bearing his name and the 
statement that he was ‘guideman, speaking eng- 
lish-franch.’ I told him I didn’t speak ‘english- 
franch,’ and didn’t want a guideman, at which he 
smiled pityingly and produced a picture-postcard 
of Saint Paul’s Cathedral. We parted without com- 
plumentr. It was nearly an hour before my heavy 
suitcase was deposited in the Agenzia of the Lloyd 
Company and I was able to rejoin the languid Basil. 

‘I’m off to Cettinje to-morrow,’ I told him, and, 


CETTINJE 189 


as I expected, he decided instantly to come too. 
Before we lunched we strolled round the little town, 
losing ourselves in tortuous streets, mere alleys be- 
tween rows of tall stone palazzi. We went into the 
dark Duomo of Saint Trifonius — Saint Trifon is 
patron of the city and his festival is on February 3d 
—and saw some beautiful carving in hidden cor- 
ners. Cattaro was a curious town, combining an 
advanced civilization with an intense discomfort. 
It had about five thousand people and a large garri- 
son; pleasure of a dull and dissipated kind was freely 
indulged in; the streets were very rowdy at night 
and the cafés stayed open late: and yet, at the same 
time, you could not in those days get even tolerable 
accommodation. The hotels were all ‘unpreten- 
tious’ in the extreme, and it was impossible, ap- 
parently, to obtain anywhere in the town a reasona- 
bly well-cooked meal. 

Basil and I made our preparations for departure 
without regret, and our preparations were charac- 
teristic of us. We had that intense dislike of each 
other’s peculiarities which is one of the great charms 
of friendship. I saw him watch with disgust as I 
filled a little envelope with bath salts and bought a 
special packet of cigarettes for the brigands. (I had 
long ago decided how I should deal with brigands. 
I should halt, take off my hat, bow, and advance 
with my cigarettes in one hand and a box of matches 
in the other. I refuse to believe that there is a 
brigand in existence who could withstand it.) Basil, 
unmindful of my laughter, talked a great deal about 
‘sticking to one’s kit as long as possible, don’t you 
know,’ and bought quantities of lozenges for his 
pop-gun. He was so superbly attired for ‘walking 


199 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


in mountainous countries’ that I began to feel 
rather ashamed of my conventional grey-flannel 
trousers. 

We started off in good style by his method, for 
which war maps and a compass were necessary. It 
did splendidly at first, until he discovered that 
something had gone radically wrong with the com- 
pass. We had wearily to retrace our steps to Cat- 
taro and after this experience Basil was all for try- 
ing to get a seat in the autobus which pants up 
the great highroad. I pointed out to him that one 
had to wire for a seat two days in advance, and we 
then succumbed to a less ambitious, ‘ psychological’ 
method of pathfinding, and studied the ground in 
search of the beaten track. It was really very easy 
to discover. There has probably for centuries been 
a constant coming and going of the Montenegrins 
between Cettinje and Cattaro. Their market is 
situated just outside the northern gate, the Porta 
Fiumara, and from here the track — the celebrated 
Scala di Montenegro —rises in zigzags up the 
precipitous side of the mountain, following the 
Fiumara Ravine. It begins by the side of the 
clear, swift Fiumara, which seems to rise full-grown 
out of nowhere, and the path is quite a good meuzx 
chemin of the Provencal type, piled over sometimes 
with stones, but zigzagging gently enough to make 
it possible for a laden mule, though hardly for a 
ridden pony. Up till 1881, I was told, it was the 
only road in existence to Cettinje. The unencum- 
bered walkers, of course, seldom follow the zigzag, 
but go almost in a bee-line up the face of the moun- 
tain. We were lucky enough to overtake a Monte- 
negrin family who were doing this, and tacked on, 


CETTINJE 191 


to them to our great advantage. The grizzled, not 
over-clean, father headed the procession and picked 
out the path. He wore the native dress underneath 
a faded jacket, obviously the product of the local 
‘American tailor,’ and carried a bulging umbrella 
under his arm. The umbrella was rather a shock, 
as I expected him to have a yataghan at least, and 
a whole collection of carving-knives in his waist- 
band. I discovered afterwards that though many 
Montenegrins dispense as much as possible with 
their weapons, none is ever seen in public without 
his umbrella. The man was followed at a respectful 
distance by his wife, who suffered in order to be. 
beautiful, for I noticed that she endured the agony 
of wearing cheap black boots instead of the native 
opanken — part sandal, part moccasin — which are 
especially adapted to climbing over rocks. After 
her came two daughters, girls of about twenty, also 
with firmly grasped umbrellas, who wore on their 
heads the little round caps of the unmarried. The 
family looked up at us as we approached, and we 
smiled at one another amiably. ‘Njegus?’ I said, 
looking a question-mark. ‘Njegus,’ replied the 
father, nodding his head and pointing. (Njegus is 
the halfway village between Cattaro and Cettinje.) 
Then followed another remark to me in Slav from 
the man, at which I shook my head, and said, 
“Inglés.” ‘Ah! Inglés!’ More smiles. 

When we had got halfway up the mountain-side, 
following the course of the torrent, we came to a 
grove of young trees and a lovely waterfall, at the 
head of which was a tiny stone mill and a bridge. 
There was a little laden mountain pony outside the 
stone hut, and within a fire of sticks was burning in 


1922 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


the middle of the floor. We crossed the stream by 
the bridge and sat and rested on a sun-warmed ledge 
or rock — evidently a recognized halting-place. 
Here the Montenegrin family accepted some of the 
wine we had brought and found it frankly horrid 
(which it was), while we ate some of their delicious 
bread and cheese. The sun poured down on us as 
we munched in silence, looking at the unsurpassed 
panorama which stretched before us. Immediately 
at our feet lay Cattaro, a tiny town huddling be- 
tween the cliff and the shore, and then, opening bay 
after bay — a series of calm blue shining lakes shut 
in by crags rising precipitously out of the water — 
the marvellous Bocche. In the far distance a line 
of glittering silver revealed the Adriatic. 

Another hour’s climb brought us on to the high- 
road, whence the walk into Njegus was easy going. 
Leaning over the parapet of the road, where the 
bridle track joined it, was a row of armed Monte- 
negrins smoking cigarettes — a frontier guard per- 
haps. I sat down on the stone to rest for a moment 
or two. It was very cold, but the air was extra- 
ordinarily clear, invigorating, and exciting. One 
could walk for ever! All round were great bare 
mountains wreathed in mist. An old woman passed 
us as we sat, bent double beneath her entire house- 
hold goods — pots, pans, clothes, and a gigantic 
lock and key of peculiar pattern — with which she 
was hurrying into Cattaro. Another passed going 
in the opposite direction, with a load of faggots on 
her back and a fowl under each arm. The Monte- 
negrin soldiers put their faces down, kissed the 
fowls, stroked their heads, and patted them with 
charming good-humour. 


CETTINJE — 193 


It was too cold to wait long, so Basil and I 
hastened along the highway till we came into the 
broad basin among the hills, in which lies Njegus 
under the shadow of Mount Lovcen. The sides of 
some of the hills, particularly of Lovcen, were 
covered with lovely trees whose leaves were now a 
rich golden brown. Others were indescribably 
desolate and bare. 

Njegus was a village in two detachments, the 
first a circle of stone huts round a broad village 
green, at the entrance to which was a new but 
modest inn, called the Grand Hotel, where you 
could buy beer and picture-postcards. This was the 
halfway house on the way to Cettinje, and your 
luggage was examined in a kind of stone stable, next 
door, surmounted by the royal arms and guarded 
by two gorgeous soldiers. Round the door of the 
inn were drawn up a number of decrepit two-horse 
cabs, which did the remaining stage of the journey 
at reduced rates. 

The inn had one big room in it, filled with 
villagers. A tall old bearded priest in one corner 
was chatting to three old ladies. He wore a high 
black cap, and black robes, with a broad band of 
purple silk round his waist, and stood with his 
umbrella held under his arm, talking volubly. 
Basil, almost as soon as he entered the place, asked 
for the bedrooms, and finding them clean, proceeded 
to unpack his kit. There was something, however, 
so bleak to me about the place, and the air was so 
invigorating, that in spite of the late hour — it was 
after four o’clock —I decided to push on to 
Cettinje. The landlord told me that another 
‘Inglés’ had just started, so I bade Basil farewell 


194 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


and hurried off in the hope of securing a travelling 
companion. 

The road is flat for a mile or two till it starts to 
climb in zigzags to the top of the great pass of 
Krivacko Zdrjelo, more than four thousand feet 
above the sea. Just before the ascent begins, and 
after passing the king’s summer palace, a plain 
yellow house by the roadside, where he was born, 
and whence his family sprang — his patronymic 
was Petrovitch Ngegosh, or ‘of Njegus’— I came 
to the second part of the village, which consisted of 
low stone huts so like the surrounding piles of stone 
as to be hard to distinguish in the gathering 
shadows. It was at this point that I met the other 
‘Inglés.’ Alas! it was more than a meeting, it was a 
recognition. He noticed that I was not a really keen 
fellow, as quickly as I spotted him to be the young 
man in a hurry. We exchanged greetings. I could 
see how my get-up (very properly) aroused his 
scorn. My rotten way of jogging on seemed to him 
ridiculous. I didn’t appear to him to have any 
earnest purpose, but seemed just cumbering up the 
place anyhow — and if we met a brigand he knew 
perfectly well he’d have to save me. He was hug- 
ging a large ‘gun’ and he could see that I hadn’t 
even a catapult. 

While we examined one another, an old woman 
emerged from a ruinous hut and saved us a mile or 
two by shouting and pointing to the track. It was 
this track, one of the roughest I can remember, and 
the gathering dark which preserved me. These, 
with the stiffness of his boots and the weight of his 
gun, crumpled up his ‘strong and silent’ constitu- 
tion. First he perspired unduly, then he ceased 


CETTINJE 195 


pawing the ground as though he disliked it, then he 
halted and remarked that after all he remembered 
he wanted to have a further look at Mount Lovcen 
—and thought he’d return to Njegus. I imagined 
him engaging Basil in conversation at dinner, and 
laughter carried me up to the top of the pass. 

The first ascent had been so rich in views that 
when I now stopped at the top to rest I was unpre- 
pared for anything finer. Yet, what I saw was al- 
most stupefying in its sheer sublimity, making me 
feel a tiny dwarf peering at some vast battlefield of 
the Elements. Looking back the way I had come, 
I could see, beyond the plateau of Njegus, the 
summits of the rocky hills I had climbed and 
crossed, and in the far distance the grey Adriatic, 
ending now ina line of crimson all along the western 
horizon. Looking towards the right, I could dimly 
see the silver gleam of Lake Scutari and in front of 
me in the direction of Nicksitch was a far-stretch- 
ing country of crags and hollows, bleak, stony, and 
closed in by mountains rising range after range, 
dark blue and mysterious in the gathering dusk. 
It was hard to imagine how any human beings 
could live in this horrific wilderness. Indeed, save 
for tiny patches of cultivation wherever a little 
earth had gathered in a cleft of the rocks, there 
seemed nothing for any one to live on, and I was 
reminded of a story that had been told to me by one 
of the officers on board ship, about the creation of 
the country. It appears that when God was making 
the world, He sent down the angel Gabriel with a 
big bag of stones to sprinkle here and there, but the 
bag unfortunately burst, and all the stones fell on 
Montenegro! 


196 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


After attempting more short cuts, one of which, 
by bringing me to the brink of a precipice, nearly 
ended my story abruptly, I stuck to the safe high- 
way. The details of the landscape now faded 
swiftly away, for there is hardly any twilight in the 
Balkans, and night fell, impenetrable and moonless. 
A curious, indescribable feeling of happiness, con- 
fidence, exhilaration now ran through me. I sang 
out loud in sheer joy of living and hurried on along 
the white, deserted road. After about an hour’s 
walking I came within sound of a party of sheep 
and goats, hurrying along, tinkling their little 
dinner-bells. A small boy detached himself from 
the custody of the flock when I caught up with it — 
I learnt afterwards that everything in Montenegro 
is done for you by small boys — took me by the 
hand, whispered something in my ear, and led me to 
a cottage with a lamp burning outside it. In the 
windowless interior there was a wooden counter on 
which stood two or three greasy black bottles, some 
glass drinking-vessels with handles, and a guttering 
candle. From behind this counter a handsome, 
rather morose young woman surveyed me deliber- 
ately. A melancholy man, with the heavy butt of a 
pistol appearing from his belt, leaned against the 
wall in one corner. On the right I noticed, through 
a rough hole in the stones, a further room, filled 
with smoke, and the sparks from a log-fire burning 
in the middle of the earthen floor. A baby, ap- 
parently sewn up in skins like a tiny mummy, was 
swinging to and fro by the side of the fire, in a kind 
of rough hammock. The morose young woman and 
I bowed ceremoniously to one another as I entered 
and she served me silently with wine, in return for 


CETTINJE 197 


which I handed her a small coin, and received back 
a most embarrassing quantity of change. We bowed 
again; the small boy once more inserted hot fingers 
in my hand, led me back into the road, and re- 
turned to his flock. This little adventure seemed 
to accentuate the sudden unreality of everything: 
I felt as though I were having curious and fantastic 
experiences in some dream from which presently 
I should awake and find myself once again in the 
twentieth century. 

I did indeed go through some such process of 
awakening when, after another hour’s walking, I 
turned a corner and saw all at once, some way be- 
low me, in the centre of a broad plain, the even rows 
of lights which I knew must be Cettinje. They 
shone in the crisp and icy air like golden stars re- 
flected in a dark-blue lake. The sight of them, how- 
ever, was inspiring; it came over me in a flash how 
hungry I was, and I reflected that I might after all 
arrive in time for dinner at the hotel. My pace 
quickened, half-an-hour went by, then an hour, but 
still the lights seemed scarcely any nearer. 

Indeed, it seemed fully two hours after catching a 
glimpse of the place that I passed thankfully under 
the first of the arc-lamps suspended over the middle 
of its broad, tree-lined boulevard. I was very far, 
even yet, from my dinner. Practically the whole of 
Cettinje was comprised in this one straight street, 
which to the tired voyager seeking the inn appeared 
to be interminable. The first house I passed was the 
palatial Embassy of the Austrian Empire. The 
town at first seemed to be deserted, but as I hurried 
on I chanced to look on my right and discovered the 
reason. Through an open doorway I could see a 


198 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


dimly-lighted parade-ground, packed with silent, 
perfectly motionless soldiers. They were dressed 
in khaki and standing at attention—I suppose 
they were being inspected. This vision of a trained 
and equipped army was startling, and quite upset 
all my preconceived notions of the Montenegrin 
mountaineer. I feared I was never to see my fancied 
brigand with his armoury of antique weapons gleam- 
ing from his decorative belt! First umbrellas, now 
khaki uniforms! The road after I passed the 
parade-ground became more animated. Gramo- 
phones blared American tunes from the numerous 
small cafés, in each of which Montenegrin ‘nuts’ 
with the correct fag-end of cigarette depending from 
their moustaches could be seen lolling across a 
billiard-table. In this part of the town there was 
considerable movement, and a constant coming and 
going of soldiers in uniform. They looked splendid 
creatures, of enormous height and girth. Among 
the officers they saluted were several unmistakable 
Russians. I thought I should have collapsed under 
the weight of my knapsack when I came at last to 
the king’s plain yellow house, and some one pointed 
out to me my inn close by. 


III 


Cettinje is on the western edge of Montenegro 
and most of the characteristic part of the country 
lies to the back of it. It had two inns, the Grand 
Hotel and the Hétel Reinwein, and they were both 
much better than anything which (in 1912) existed 
at Cattaro. Comfortable and clean and in the hands 
of persons well-disposed towards the English, it was 
hardly surprising that they were visited by a steady 


CETTINJE 199 


stream of tourists. If the country had been a little 
more ‘settled,’ the Turk a little farther away, there 
seemed no reason why coloured placards of *Cet- 
tinje for health and pleasure’ should not decorate 
our railway stations. Those who revel in ‘native 
costumes’ could hardly be happier than in Cettinje: 
indeed, just as I was entering the inn, an English 
young woman remarked in ecstasies, ‘Look, mo- 
ther, look; here’s the real type!’ as a Montenegrin 
brave walked past her. 

The sights of the little red-roofed town were 
easily seen. It lay, like Njegus, in a broad, com- 
paratively fertile plain shut in by bare stone hills, 
and the town was almost all contained in the one 
long street, the broad Katunska Ulica. The houses 
were little, low, two-storied buildings, all of differ- 
ent colours — green, mauve, pink, drab, light blue. 
Here and there was a larger house; and one preten- 
tious art nouveau villa, built right on the street, was 
pointed out to me as actually belonging to a Monte- 
negrin subject. I did not admire it. The other big 
houses were the Embassies, and both those of 
Austria and Russia were so large as to put in the 
shade the king’s modest home. The Russian Em- 
bassy was particularly imposing. It was pleasant 
to note that our own Embassy, which was being 
completed at the time of my visit, did not make 
this error of taste, but was a pleasant, long, low 
building well in keeping with its surroundings, and 
no larger than the palace near which it stood. It 
was rather a shock, though, to discover that we had 
no Minister resident in this the centre of all Balkan 
intrigues. For five years, they told me, since the 
departure of Mr. Kennedy, there had been no 


200 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


British Consul or representative in the country. 
Mr. Kennedy had evidently made himself well 
known and respected all over Montenegro during 
his term of office, for he was still remembered and 
spoken of. “Why can’t you send us back Mr. 
Kennedy?’ was the remark one heard everywhere. 
It was surprising to find how many of the peas- 
ants spoke excellent American. Apparently it had 
long been the custom for the young Montenegrin to 
seek his fortune in the United States. In 1911, they 
told me, when King Nicholas was forced to issue a 
degree restricting emigration, there were as many 
as twenty-five thousand of his subjects in America. 
The fearful poverty of their fatherland was so evi- 
dent on every side that it was hardly to be wondered 
at that many of the young men went abroad to seek 
their fortunes. Most of the people I spoke to seemed 
to have a great liking and respect for the English, 
particularly as individuals; but it was somewhat of 
a jar to discover that the decay of our national 
prestige was everywhere discussed, and taken as a 
matter of course, in this remote mountain village. 
An ancient convent with a church containing the 
tombs of the Petrovich dynasty, situated on the 
wooded slope of a hill; various Government build- 
ings, barracks and ammunition factories; the houses 
of the Crown Prince and of Prince Mirko; an excel- 
lent girls’ boarding-school, founded by the Empress 
Feodorowna of Russia; a small stucco ‘theatre’ like 
a Welsh conventicle, used both for the meetings of 
the Deputies and for inferior cinematograph shows 
— these made up the little round of ‘sights.’ Op- 
posite the king’s new palace was the old palace or 
Biljardo — a long, two-storied building, painted a 


CETTINJE 201 


bright red, which was used partly as a grammar- 
school and partly for other Governmental purposes. 
It received its name from its billiard-table, the 
porterage of which up the ‘Montenegrin’s ladder’ 
from Cattaro was such a terrific business that it had 
never been forgotten! 

It did not take me very long to get the hang of 
Cettinje, but the bleakness of it made at first a 
rather ungracious impression. It was, however, 
clean and healthy, civilized and well-managed. An 
air of good government and of safety of life and 
limb, an atmosphere of conscious rectitude per- 
vaded it. There were none of the dangers here 
which make life thrilling in the outskirts of Paris or 
in a Welsh mining village. I could not sit and dream 
at all in this quaint little ‘dream city.’ Its air of 
virtue —and what a magnificent air it was! — 
made one anxious to do things, to climb mountains, 
to undergo hardships. 

At the time of my visit King Nicholas’ ultimatum 
to Turkey was on the point of being presented, and 
the hotel was very full of foreigners. One of them, 
an Englishman who had lived for a year or more in 
the country, told me many things about the little 
town which made it seem more interesting. He told 
me of the skilful diplomacy of King Nicholas, by 
which he set off one great Power against another, 
played Austria off against Russia and used Italy as 
a make-weight. I saw King Nicholas one morning 
exercising a fine grey horse in the little park at the 
back of his house —a giant of a man, of a vast 
breadth and weight, and with a fiery eye. My Eng- 
lish friend informed me that the chief faults of the 
Montenegrins were their inordinate vanity and 


202 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


their gambling propensities. He told me a great 
deal about the blood feuds, pursued for generation 
after generation, and only to be ended by the last 
survivors of the warring houses throwing stones 
ceremonially into the waters of a river, up to the 
number of the slain. He pointed out to me men in 
the town whose time, sooner or later, would come. 
They put their affairs, if they had any, in good 
order and waited fatalistically till the hour when a 
little puff of white smoke from behind a boulder 
should mark the gunshot which would send them to 
their account. It was the law of the Vendetta. 

I was disappointed in not seeing any of the 
guslars, the troubadours who, as with all primitive 
peoples, go about the country singing the exploits 
of their great heroes. ‘The gusla, 1 was led to un- 
derstand, is a kind of rough violin with one string 
to it, on which horrible wailing noises are perpe- 
trated by a short curved bow. The singing to which 
it is an accompaniment is said to be in a mournful 
minor key. 

I never got tired of watching the Montenegrins 
stalking majestically up and down their broad 
street, swinging their strukas (shawls) from their 
shoulders, or showing their white teeth in their 
indolent smile. The excitement in the place was 
considerable: the soldiers, in smart uniforms, 
crowded the street, and I was told that their equip- 
ment and the matériel supplied to their army by 
Russia were of the most modern and up-to-date 
kind. The king appeared to be immensely popular 
~—a true father and leader of his people, whom he 
had several times led to victory against the Turks, 
notable in the war which ended in 1876, just when 


CETTINJE 203 


he was on the point of bombarding Scutari. It has 
been said that all the Montenegrins are gentlemen, 
and that after the great defeat of Kossovo, in which 
the Turks destroyed the Empire of the Serbs, in the 
middle of the fourteenth century, the Serbian no- 
bility fled to the Montenegrin heights, where they 
have been established ever since, and maintained 
their freedom during five centuries of unremitting 
warfare, until they were shamefully robbed of it by 
their allies in 1919. The old king was a celebrated 
Slav poet and wrote many plays and poems, of 
which the chief is the well-known play ‘The Em- 
press of the Balkans,’ which was first produced in 
May of 1885. 

It was all very interesting and I was obsessed by 
the thought that I ought to be finding it so. Yet I 
could not get away from the conviction that, do 
what I would, Cettinje was a disappointment. If 
I am to enjoy wildness, I suppose I must have 
warmth. Some squalid, low-lying, ill-governed, 
villainous Turkish town with bazaars and sweet 
coffee and cigarettes and wickedness would have 
appealed to me far more. 

Indeed, the only thing about Cettinje which 
roused me to enthusiasm was the magnificent pa- 
triotism of the king and his subjects, and their whole- 
hearted devotion to their country. My friend in the 
hotel lent me an interesting book about the country 
by an American writer, Mr. Russell, which con- 
tained a rough translation of the national ballad or 
piésma, made for his people by King Nicholas. The 
thought, if not the actual words, ran in my head as 
I left the little mountain capital on foot for Riecka. 

Mr. Russell’s translation of the ballad, which be- 


204 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


gins, “‘Onamo, Onamo, Za br’'da’ (‘Out there, out 
there beyond the mountains’), runs as follows: 


Out there, out there beyond the mountains, 
My Czar has ceased to speak, they say; 

Of heroes was his speech that day. 

Out there, out there beyond the mountains, 
In some dark cave beneath the hill, 

They say my Czar is sleeping still. 

He wakes! and rising in our wrath, 

We'll hurl the proud usurper forth. 

From Dechan’s sainted race awakes 

A single prayer within that shrine, 

And Paradise is surely mine! 


Out there, out there beyond the mountains, 
Where the blue sky to heaven her light 
Is breaking — brothers to the fight! 


Out there, out there beyond the mountains, 
Where tramps the foaming steed of war, 
Old Jugo calls his sons afar 

To aid! to aid! — in my old age 

Defend me from the foeman’s rage. 


Out there, out there beyond the mountains, 
My children, follow one and all 

Where Nikola, your Prince, doth call: 

And steep anew in Turkish gore 

The sword Czar Dushan flashed of yore, 
Out there, out there beyond the mountains! 


II 


ANTIVARI 
I 


Wirnovut doubt there was something in the biting 
air of Cettinje which spurred me to energy, to 
feats, indeed, of athleticism on which I can only 
look back in alarm and wonder how I did them. 
Once more scorning the stuffy little motor-omnibus 
— there was one which plied between Cettinje and 
Podgoritza — I set out at seven o’clock, on a misty 
day, to walk to Riecka, with a vague idea that I 
should be able to get from there to Antivari. I 
heard one could get a steamer across the lake from 
Riecka to Virpasar, also that there was a light rail- 
_way from Virpasar to Antivari: but no one seemed 
to be sure if the two ‘corresponded.’ 

The road to Riecka starts behind the theatre at 
Cettinje and passes the hospital, which was empty 
enough when I saw it, though a few weeks later it 
was to be full to overflowing with wounded men. 
As I walked, the mist gradually cleared off, and by 
the time I reached the top of the mountain ridge at 
a point called Bella Vista the sky was perfectly 
clear and the view extraordinary. Far below, among 
the dark hills, one could see the veiled silver of Lake 
Scutari, bounded on the Albanian side by the ‘ac- 
cursed mountains,’ the Procleti Gori of Albania, 
whose peaks have the appearance of ebony, topped 
with a filmy cap of snow; while on the right the 
distant Adriatic could be divined rather than recog- 
nized. 


206 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


The road after this point sinks steeply by the 
side of bare Karst hills, with the lovely and fertile 
valley of Dobrskoselo a thousand feet below it on 
the right, and after many zigzags reaches Riecka. 
I was often able to cut off considerable distances by | 
clambering down among worn rocks, but the foot- 
paths and the main road were alike thronged with 
people. Laden donkeys picked their way up and 
down the steep tracks, while stores and material 
required by the military authorities were being 
dragged up and down the road, in a constant suc- 
cession of rude wagons. The women, of whom 
there were crowds, often seemed to be carrying as 
heavy burdens as the donkeys, while the men, very 
indolent and handsome, seldom carried anything 
besides their weapons and the perpetual cigarette. 
I was much amused, just after beginning the final 
descent into Riecka, by a little group of boys who 
passed me on their way to school. Each had his 
large black umbrella under his arm; two of the 
smallest were smoking cigarettes, and the rest were 
saying aloud their French verbs. They laughed at 
me and said ‘Good-bye’ in English as I passed. 

As I left the highlands and got farther down 
the valley, the temperature grew correspondingly 
warmer. After Cettinje to walk into Riecka was 
like entering a conservatory, and the whole country- 
side altered accordingly, growing less rugged and 
more fertile and luxuriant. The lower slopes of the 
hills were thickly wooded, and there were many fine 
fig trees by the rushing trout stream in the centre 
of the valley. 

Riecka turned out to be an enchanting little 
town, chiefly consisting of one long street of small 


ANTIVARI 207 


houses facing the river, with shops on the ground 
floor, under green-painted wooden balconies. At 
the entrance of the village some fascinating bakeries 
and cookshops, all open to the public gaze, re- 
minded me, by the delicious odour which came 
from them, how fearfully hungry I was. The river 
which the picturesque street looked on to was the 
trout stream of a kilometre or two back grown sud- 
denly big —a delightful, clear, tree-shaded river, 
crossed by two very handsome stone bridges, under 
which the big trout and the scoranje, for which the 
place is famous, shelter from the sun. By the 
time I reached Riecka the sun was already very 
strong, as strong as an English midsummer, and the 
scene was sparkling and delicious. The broad road 
between the houses and the river was quite a 
thronged promenade, all the people, of both sexes, 
wearing the native costume. Four or five very 
handsome boys took charge of me as I loitered on 
the old bridge looking down at the fish in the clear 
water. One of them, taller than the rest, with a 
bold head, flashing brown eyes, and an olive com- 
plexion of great loveliness, took me across to an old 
woman resting under a tree with a basket under her 
arm covered with large green leaves. She uncovered 
the basket when I drew near and the boys filled all 
my pockets as full as they could hold with luscious 
green figs. The simple-minded old creature wanted 
to give me change out of a piece of ten centesimi 
which she selected from the handful of coins I of- 
fered her. I do not think I have ever tasted any- 
thing in my life so ambrosial as those figs, after my 
three-hour tramp from Cettinie. 

The boys insisted on accompanying me along 


208 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


the highway to the spot, about a mile and a half 
farther on, where the river suddenly widened and I 
saw the little wheezy grey steamer moored to a pier, 
which was to take me down to Lake Scutari and 
across to Virpasar. On the way the brown-eyed boy 
picked me an immense bunch of mauve-pink cycla- 
men which — when I reached the white stucco 
house which contained the booking-office — he pre- 
sented to me with a charming smile. I am con- 
vinced that the Montenegrin small boy has the 
nicest manners of any one in the world! 

Inside the dépét I discovered that the steamer 
would not start for over an hour, that it ran in 
conjunction with the train from Virpasar to Anti- 
vari, and that the fare for the entire journey by 
train and steamer amounted to only four corone. 
The young man who gave me this information wore 
elegant clothes in the Birmingham style (self- 
measurement: fit and cut guaranteed) and a becom- 
ing fez. He had a pale face and heavy Oriental eyes 
and spoke admirable French. He took a well-bred 
lack of interest in me, and was evidently a travelled 
person in an advanced state of civilization, for I 
saw a novel by Willy open on his desk. 

Opposite the dépédt was the most primitive 
kafana I had yet seen even in Montenegro. A 
wooden roof supported by four wooden posts 
covered some ant-eaten chairs and tables which, on 
three sides, were open to the winds. The proprietor 
leaned against one of the posts, guarding a shelf 
with about a dozen eggs on it in a row, some bread, 
and a large cheese. A few cases of beer and bottles 
of rakia were under the shelf. There were six or 
seven unshaven, idle, shiftless-looking natives sit- 


ANTIVARI 209 


ting at the tables when I entered. They eyed me 
with sleepy curiosity and seemed impressed when I 
ordered a bottle of Austrian beer — which turned 
out to be excellent. It was much better than the 
hard-boiled egg and sour bread which I bought to 
eat with it, and in the warm sunlight it induced a 
delicious sleepiness. An old blue diligence drove up, 
as I crossed the road to explore the steamer, which 
seemed of an immemorial antiquity and was 
crowded with peasants all carrying immense untidy 
packages. From which of the villages it had come 
I did not discover. 

I found I had still more than an hour before the 
steamer would start, so I lay on my back in the hot 
sun, on the inclined wooden slope which led down 
to the boat, watching out of half-closed eyes the 
fish jump in the shadows under the trees or the light 
dancing on the dark green water. It seemed impos- 
sible to believe that only a week before a whole 
battalion of Turkish soldiers, embarked on londras 
(native sailing-vessels from Scutari, about the size 
of fishing-smacks), had been ambushed and massa- 
cred a few miles from here, and that their bodies 
were now probably bobbing about in the lake! 
Such, however, was the story I had heard repeated 
everywhere at Cettinje, and the sight a little later 
of some red fezzes floating in the water in part con- 
firmed it. 

I was falling peacefully asleep when the little 
steamer —it was called the Danitza — suddenly 
emitted an ear-splitting hoot, and I jumped hur- 
riedly to my feet and rushed on board just in time. 
Never before had I been on such a shaky, dirty 
little tub. Before the lake was reached we had still 


210 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


some miles of the river to traverse, the passage 
among the tangled masses of water-lilies being 
marked out for us by rough wooden stakes. In 
spite of their help so sudden were the turns and so 
bad the steering that we several times nearly ran 
aground. On either side of the river were steep hills, 
with loftier mountains looming up at the back of 
them. Every now and then we passed a little 
cluster of round huts. Black punts, which rose to 
high points at bow and stern, were moored against 
the bank near them. Several villages I noticed 
seemed utterly ruined and deserted, as though they 
might recently have been sacked by an invader. 
There was no sign of any human being, but occa- 
sionally on the marshy ground at the water’s edge, 
with its beautiful neck curved like a question-mark, 
a crane made a white dot against the green of the 
hillside. At last we turned our final corner, and the 
broad expanse of the lake, fringed with marshes full 
of duck, pelicans, and cranes, lay ahead of us. 
This first view of Lake Scutari is quite unforget- 
table. It seems to stretch to infinity save where, 
here and there on the horizon, the black crests of the 
mountains, under a halo of snow, appear, towering in 
an unreal way above the clouds. After passing an 
island covered with houses and fortifications, the 
fortress and arsenal of Lesandria guarding the mouth 
of the river, and the twin-peaked island of Vranjina, 
and steaming for half an hour or so across the open 
water, we turned up a canal which wound amid 
marshy fields to the village of Virpasar. This vil- 
lage, for the terminus of a railway, was far from 
imposing. It boasted a little round stone watch- 
tower, from which doubtless, for centuries past, 


ANTIVARI 211 


anxious eyes had scanned the lake in the direction 
of Scutari for the advance of the Turk; it had also a 
pink shed which served as a railway station. These, 
with a bridge, and a circle of stone cottages facing 
an open market-place, were all there was of it. The 
village was surrounded by marshes filled with wild 
fowl, about which cows roamed with small boys in 
pursuit of them. It had secured an unenviable 
notoriety among travellers for its evening concert 
of frogs, and for the deadly fever mists which rise 
at sunset from the swamps. In the station yard I 
found two Turks sitting placidly on their heels, like 
tailors, and doing business together. Nothing could 
have been more peaceful or contented than their 
appearance, or more primitive. Virpasar was not, 
however, nearly so uncivilized as it first appeared 
to one at least of the hungry collection of tramps 
and brigands who got off the Danitza. Inside the 
pink station shed was served a luncheon of such 
surpassing excellence, combined with cheapness, 
that its memory Time, with me at all events, has 
not obliterated. About a dozen people, in shiny 
European clothes — Italians, Germans, and Aus- 
trians — collected to eat it; the majority of them, I 
fancy, were going by the bigger steamer to Plav- 
nitza, en route for Podgoritza. We began with eggs, 
followed by a mayonnaise of lake trout, continued 
with a tender chicken, and concluded with an un- 
usually delicious sweet, while the charge was three 
corone. I had not eaten such a meal since leaving 
Ragusa, and the only explanation of it that I could 
think of was that the authorities realized what was 
necessary to prepare the voyager for the Monte- 
negrin railway. 


212 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


II 

There was, and perhaps there still is, only one 
line of railway in Montenegro, namely that between 
Virpasar and Antivari, though a line was ‘pro- 
jected’ to Riecka, and thence through Podgoritza 
up the Zeta Valley to Nicksitch. From Virpasar to 
Antivari is a distance of twenty-seven and one half 
miles, but to get from one place to the other a 
mountain barrier three thousand feet high has to be 
negotiated, so that the little railway, all things con- 
sidered, really did the country great credit. 

The train into which I climbed, after draining the 
last drop of the luscious Turkish coffee which com- 
pleted my luncheon, consisted of three carriages, 
drawn by a little dark red engine. There were only 
two other passengers and they looked more like 
Albanians than Montenegrins. I had plenty of 
chances of observing them, for out of all the other 
compartments they chose mine to sit in. I confess I 
could have wished, when I saw their formidable 
cutlasses, pistols, and other weapons, that things 
had fallen otherwise. 

We trundled along to the head of the fertile 
valley, and then began our zigzag ascent of the flank 
of the mighty Rumija, doubling and redoubling on 
our tracks, and rising in greater loops while the air 
grew steadily cooler. At last we reached the end of 
our climb, the guard came in and shut all the win- 
dows and lit an evil-smelling oil lamp, and with a 
shrill hoot from the engine the train dived into the 
tunnel. This tunnel is constructed just under the 
celebrated Sutormann Pass, across which, in the 
seventies of the last century, the Montenegrins 
fought their way heroically to the sea. The passage 


ANTIVARI 213 


of the tunnel took a long time and I was glad of the 
lamp, for my Albanian fellow passengers, who sat 
nursing their pistols and their yataghans, did not 
inspire confidence. 
_ The train drew up, as soon as it emerged into 
daylight, at the usual little pink house. This one 
bore a number on its side, like the lodges on Italian 
railway lines, while the name Gytopmah, in Cyrillic 
characters, above its front door, announced a seem- 
ingly non-existent village. While the engine paused 
to recover from the strain of dragging itself three 
thousand feet up into the clouds (or else to allow 
the guard to kiss his mother, which he did with 
great enthusiasm), I stumbled out on to the per- 
manent way in the hope of discovering where I was. 
In heaven, evidently— suspended like great Jove 
in mid-air, upon a cloud! This was the first 1mpres- 
sion. London eyes, accustomed to narrow slits of 
streets and the subtleties of tiny urban landscapes, 
may perhaps be forgiven for not taking in more at a 
time. For it was indeed a stupendous view; we had 
pierced the mountain and had emerged trium- 
phantly on the other side. I found myself standing 
on the top, as it were, of a great amphitheatre of 
hills which sloped gradually down to a wide bay, 
lapped by the gleaming waters of the Adriatic. 
Immediately below me white clouds clung like 
gauze to the hillside. The whole of the middle 
distance was much less distinct than the land which 
fringed the sea, the details of which I could distin- 
guish clearly. On the left of the bay jutted out a 
low, reddish promontory sheltering a roadstead, in 
which about a dozen dots of vessels lay at anchor. 
Some large houses shone up white against this head- 


214 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


land and faced the shipping, but there was no trace 
of a town. Still, this, I knew, must be Antivari. On 
the flat meadowland which fringed the shore, al- 
most in the centre of the bay, was another white 
building, which I guessed to be the Crown Prince’s 
country villa, Topolica. Far round on the right, 
facing the headland of Antivari (the Punta Volo- 
vica), was another headland, fortified, which I 
found afterwards looked down on what was then 
the Austrian port of Spizza. 

Save for the bitter cold, I would gladly have 
spent an hour in this eyrie among the clouds. 
During my wanderings in a country all of which is 
in the grand manner, I had so far come across no 
view so curiously thrilling. One could imagine how 
a conquering army which had fought its way over 
the pass would be rewarded by it. I don’t suppose 
even Xenophon’s ten thousand Greeks could have 
shouted more joyously “Thalassa! Thalassa!’ than 
those intrepid Montenegrins of half-a-century ago. 
I turned to look back at the barrier of rock which 
the invading army must have surmounted. A few 
hundred feet above me was the great frowning 
fortress —battered now and half-ruinous — which 
had proved unable to guard the pass. I noticed 
that there was another fortress, almost in a straight 
line with it, immediately below me, and below that 
a third —a whole series of shattered fortifications 
which must have been the scenes of bloody and 
desperate fighting. 

A cry from the guard, who had now withdrawn 
himself from the maternal embrace, brought my 
reverie to a hasty end, and I hurried back to find 
the train had evidently been kept waiting for me. 


ANTIVARI Q15 


The Albanian brigands scowled: but we had to stop 
again, though, because a number of the guard’s mo- 
ther’s hens were discovered established comfortably 
in the first-class carriage. When they had been dis- 
lodged we began once more our laborious descent. 
We passed, winding and twisting, through grey-green 
olive groves, fragrant and melancholy, and waited 
interminably at little villages nestling under wooded 
spurs of hill, that had been imvisible from the 
heights. At last, after a journey which seemed ri- 
diculously long for what had appeared to be the 
distance, we reached the belt of level ground which 
runs all round the bay, and the engine turned, pant- 
ing, towards the left and hurried in the direction of 
the shipping and the white hotel. A mile, and then, 
after passing some recently burnt goods-sheds that 
were still smoking and warm, we stopped with a 
jerk at the station of Antivari. 


Til 


A squalid line of Turkish cottages, darkened by 
trees, greeted me as I left the station yard for the 
highway; a few better buildings were encountered 
when I reached the sea-front, among them the 
Austrian Consulate; and then on the left, on the 
slope of the headland, about fifty feet above the 
level of the road, was the astonishing hotel. I knew 
vaguely before I started that there was an hotel of 
some kind at Antivari, but I had certainly never 
expected anything like this. Imagine it! A suave 
manager greeted me in the hall in perfect English, 
in a manner that recalled one of the more prosper- 
ous caravanserais in his native Switzerland. He 
handed me over to a neat maid in white apron and 


216 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


black alpaca, who led me to my room, a room dis- 
tinguished, in a semi-civilized country, for the 
confort moderne referred to on the establishment’s 
exquisite notepaper. I opened my eyes wider and 
wider, and wondered if it could really all be true, 
and who it was who had established — surely for 
his own amusement — this oasis of comfort in a 
wilderness of stone huts. In the music-room I dis- 
covered to my delight an English newspaper not 
more than a week old, one or two battered Tauch- 
nitz novels, and the inevitable Englishman. The 
Englishman was of the usual enigmatic type that 
lives happily and contentedly in any remote corner 
of the globe. His hair and moustache were a little 
whiter than iron-grey, and his face was dark red 
and deeply furrowed with wrinkles, and he wore a 
suit of dingy white flannel, with the sleeves of the 
coat rolled up to the elbow, and a pair of battered 
tennis-shoes. A kind of sombrero lay on the table 
by his side. He waited for me to speak to him first, 
but when I did so proved the soul of amiability, and 
offered at once to show me all the sights. We went 
out together and explored, and he certainly proved 
a most interesting guide. 

Modern Antivari, it appeared, was the work of an 
Italian company which had obtained a concession 
some years before from the Montenegrin Govern- 
ment to carry out harbour works and to build the 
railway. It was this company which had con- 
structed the fine hotel and its annexe, chiefly for 
the housing of its two hundred employees, and had 
made the stone quay or molo alongside which the 
Lloyd steamer and the weekly boat from Bari were © 
accustomed to be moored. The company had also 


ANTIVARI 217 


built a number of sheds and antrepéts, and made a 
great show of energy. But just as its marvellous 
little railway carried hardly any merchandise, and 
only a very few passengers, so in 1912, the beauti- 
ful harbour was doing hardly any trade. (Cattaro, 
of course, took very good care that it shouldn’t!) So 
great was the hatred then existing between Antivari 
and Cattaro that a Wednesday boat from Antivari, 
out of pure cussedness, timed its departure just to 
miss the big steamer which started from Cattaro for 
Trieste on that day. If you wanted to go from 
Antivari to Cattaro on any day except Wednesday 
or Friday you had to walk, and along a road of 
which great stretches were still only ‘projected.’ 
The Italian company established on the Punta 
Volovica looked across the great bay to the Aus- 
trian fort above Spizza, saying as clearly as its big 
hotel could speak it: ‘Thus far and no farther!’ 

A few feluccas of the kind that hoist lateen- 
shaped sails of many and brilliant colours, a bat- 
tered green barque of the most aged and decrepit 
appearance, the Crown Prince’s little yacht, and 
an Italian tramp steamer were the only vessels in 
the roadstead while I was there. Antivari seemed 
an undecided place, the unquiet grave of a variety 
of projects. Once it was put forward that its lovely 
climate and glorious surroundings would fit it for 
exploitation as a gambling-hell, but, to his credit be 
it said, King Nicholas sternly vetoed this sugges- 
tion, with the remark that Monte Negro and Monte 
Carlo were two different places. Meanwhile the 
effort to make it into a port was being courageously 
continued by the company, though of real trade 
and activity there appeared to be but little. The 


218 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


occasional motorist and the tourist waiting for the 
Friday steamer —and these only during certain 
months in the year—were its sole visible sources 
of profit, and they scarcely served to mask the 
politicians in the background. 

After exploring the port of Antivari, my com- 
panion and I walked inland up the highroad for 
some distance, and then branched off along a path 
through olive groves till we came to the dilapidated 
town of Bar, or ‘Old’ Antivari, which les about 
three kilometres from the sea. My Englishman 
carried a fat and dilapidated black umbrella under 
his arm, like a native, and talked volubly all the 
time. ‘Old’ Antivari was a great contrast to its 
modern namesake, and was quite untroubled by 
any dreams of expansion. It seemed so old that per- 
haps it knew all about ‘progress’ and its futility. 
Approaching it through the mournful olive groves 
you came first to the great fortress, which originally 
was in itself ‘Old’ Antivari, for its massive walls 
sheltered a complete town, the foundation of which 
local gossip attributed to the Phcenicians. I should 
not have known a Phcenician town if I saw one, and 
Baedeker was silent on the subject — indeed, he is 
far from expansive about any of the coast between 
Cattaro and Dulcigno — so I could not vouch for 
the Phcenicians. Probably most of the old masonry 
on this coast was the work of the great island re- 
public, for the Venetians had to bestir themselves 
to maintain their Eastern Empire against the forces 
of Islam, and built countless fortresses. The strong- 
hold was battered almost to bits in the war of 1874, 
and left in ruins for many years. It was now used 
as an arsenal, and no one was allowed inside save 


ANTIVARI 219 


with a special permit from the Minister of War at 
Cettinje. (A disastrous explosion occurred here in 
December, 1912, two months after my visit, in 
which large stores of ammunition were destroyed.) 

The newer town of ‘Old’ Antivari, built outside 
the castle walls, was, curiously enough, almost in- 
visible from them. It lay in a hollow surrounded by 
an olive grove, and you came suddenly on it, down 
a steep incline. The place presented very much the 
appearance of a Turkish village, and on either side 
of the main street were Turkish bazaars, where in- 
ferior Austrian goods could be purchased at en- 
hanced prices. Turkish faces stared from the dark 
penetralia of their shops at the intruding infidel. 
On Mondays and Fridays, the market days, the 
presence of veiled Turkish women in the streets 
lent the scene some of the charm and mystery of the 
East. There were two mosques at Antivari, and 
also a Roman Catholic Archbishop (and, presuma- 
bly, a cathedral, though I did not see it). Its 
Turkish aspect is easily understood when it is re- 
membered that the town had belonged to Monte- 
negro only since 1878, and that it was situated but 
fourteen miles northwest of Scutari. I was told that 
the greater part of its twelve hundred inhabitants 
were Turks, for all the territory from the seaward 
side of the mountains to the coast is fertile, and was 
tilled principally by Turks, who are far better 
farmers than the Montenegrins. I wish now that I 
had spent longer exploring this curious place, but 
the unexpected confort moderne of my hotel had 
momentarily gone to my head. I hurried back with 
my companion to study the wine list, and to order 
myself the most exotic apéritif that I could find, 


220 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


preparatory to indulging in what the hotel note- 
paper described as a bain de luze. 

I found ‘Mahree,’ the pretty Bosnian chamber- 
maid, laying out my hairbrushes and pyjamas when 
I got to my room. She smiled at me with the assur- 
ance of a general favourite, sat on my bed, and told 
me in pretty broken English that she had been in 
Chicago, that she liked Americans, and that her 
father lived in Trieste, where she had been born. 
She showed her white teeth in an engaging smile, 
and wherever she got her pretty frock from, in that 
forlorn wilderness, Heaven only knows. She had 
been at Antivari for a year, she said, and ‘Madame’ 
was fearful strict: but she liked the old Englishman, 
‘Oh, awful much, for he is so goot.’ She did not 
explain exactly in what his ‘gootness’ lay, though I 
was quite prepared to believe in it, and what she 
said inflamed my curiosity about him. We dined 
together, the Englishman and I, at the same table, 
in the elegant salle-d-manger. It was an epicurean 
feast, while the cellar of the hotel proved equal to 
anything I had met with since leaving Trieste. 
‘“Mahree’ waited on us, and eyed us both with in- 
terest as we ate. 

The Englishman had landed, he said, three 
months ago, from one of the Austrian-Lloyd steam- 
ers, and had liked the situation of the hotel so much 
that he had stayed on ever since. Evidently he had 
lived in tropical countries; his eye for crops, for 
possible exports and imports, and the peculiar 
intonation of his voice, made it likely that he had 
been a trader; while his disgust when he discovered 
that my red Meredith was not a Baedeker (as he 
had hoped) suggested a complete indifference to the 


ANTIVARI 221 


Muses. He was exceptionally well-informed and 
patently uncultured; he was old; his time was ap- 
parently his own; and he spoke no language save 
English. Though his interest in his surroundings 
appeared to be of the liveliest kind, yet the only 
thing which, so far as I could gather, excited his 
heartfelt enthusiasm was a certain ‘temperance’ 
hotel in Princes Street, Edinburgh! Was he a spy 
in the pay of the Austrian Government, a remit- 
tance man, a fugitive from justice, or just a wan- 
derer? I dismissed my various conjectures, one 
after another, in favour of the last. He was simply 
a born wanderer, like the man in Kipling’s ‘Sestina 
of the Tramp Royal.’ He was just the kind of per- 
son to say, in the same accent: ‘Pay couldn’t ’old 
me when my time was done.’ Here he was, for half 
a year, at Antivari. Next year might see him in a 
Greek village, in some cheap but comfortable Swiss 
hotel, or in a South American town looking out for 
commercial openings. This, I guessed, in Kipling’s 
words, would be his view of things: 

It’s like a book, I think, this bloomin’ world, 

Which you can read and care for just so long, 

But presently you feel that you will die 

Unless you get the page you’re readin’ done, 


And turn another — likely not so good; 
But what you’re after is to turn ’em all.
