BUDUA 
I 
‘You cannot get to Cattaro on foot, sir,’ said the 
manager of the hotel, showing his little white teeth. 
‘There is no conveyance, no road; you must wait 
for the steamer. There is a local boat on Wednes- 
day afternoon, or you can wait for the Lloyd 
steamer on Friday.’ ‘I’m sorry,’ I said, ‘but I must 
catch the midday boat on Wednesday from Cat- 
taro, and I propose to get there, somehow.’ This 
seemed to have a good effect, for, seeing that in one 
way or another I was determined to reach my des- 
tination, he helped me by getting the Ordnance 
Map of the coast of the Primorje, on which I could 
pick out the track. From Antivari to Spizza was 
but a two-hours walk; and from Spizza to Budua, 
where I proposed to sleep, there was a clear track 
indicated and, most of the way, the highroad. Of 
the excellence of the Austrians as road-builders I 
had already had experience. From Budua I dis- 
covered I could get on to Cattaro by the diligence. 

I was quite easy in my own mind as to the possi- 
bility of getting to Budua, but it was astonishing 
that neither guide-books nor any one at Antivari 
could give me any information about the place. 

I woke early on the morning of my departure, to 
hear the rain falling with melancholy persistence on 
the gravel outside my bedroom window. It was the 
kind of soft rain that goes on and on. Come what 
might, I had to face it, and before eight o’clock I 


BUDUA 223 


was attempting to find the short cut through the 
gardens of the Crown Prince’s villa, which the 
genial Swiss had indicated to me. To add to the 
bitterness of my chagrin I went nearly a mile along 
the wrong side of the canal, getting myself wet to 
the skin in brushing through the shrubs which over- 
hung the track, in the process, and had to return 
and begin again the other side. When at last I 
reached the gates of the Villa Topolica — across the 
flat waste of lands by the shore — they were not 
only locked but wired with barbed wire. However, 
by turning inland towards the main road I found 
the main gate, with a sentry-box by the side of it; 
and this I was able to open. The grounds of the 
villa consisted of several acres and were prettily laid 
out, and crossed by those soft gravel carriage-roads 
which are a feature of private parks, probably all 
over the world. It was one thing to get into this 
nice garden, another to get out of it. However, at 
last, in a far corner I found a gap through which I 
could climb, avoiding the barbed wire. I was not 
free even yet; on the other side of the hedge was a 
swift brook running out into the sea. Luckily, just 
before its waters merged with the salt water, there 
was a spot narrow enough for a jump, and after 
negotiating this the rest was easy. All the same, that 
short cut was the longest and most nerve-racking I 
have ever attempted, and if I have to walk from 
Antivari to Spizza again I shall take care to go by 
the highway. The Prince’s villa was almost on the 
last inch of Montenegrin soil, and it was probably 
by jumping that brook that I landed myself in what 
was then Austria. 

The track led round a small headland on which, 


Q24 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


finely situated, were the ruins of a castle. A board 
with a notice in Slavonic characters was obviously 
to the effect that trespassers would be prosecuted, 
but since I could not read it I was not deterred. The 
castle had evidently had a terrific battering at one 
time or another. Perhaps it was used to store am- 
munition or for some military purpose, for a tiny 
flag was tied to a stick on top of one of the towers 
which had been roughly patched with stone and the 
entrance to which was locked. 

It was very interesting to poke about among the 
ruins and to look down through great cracks in the 
masonry at the tranquil sea far beneath. The most 
complete part of the ruins was the chapel, which 
had a lovely floor of black and white marble. And 
from the higher points on to which one could climb, 
what a splendid view there was, in spite of the rain! 
One could see the broad bay with the white villa in 
the midst of it and the white hotel shining on the 
eastern headland; and, at the back, rising at first 
gradually, then with almost precipitous steepness, 
the cloud-hung Montenegrin mountains culminat- 
ing in the mighty Rumija. The paths and all the 
soil about here were a curious brick-red colour, as 
though soaked in centuries of blood; and against 
this redness the silver and grey-green olive trees 
made an exquisite contrast. 

If one did not look at the frowning mountains at 
the back, only at the sea, the well-kept shady path 
and the lovely vegetation near at hand, one might 
have fancied oneself in Devonshire rather than the 
Near East, so gracious was the aspect of the coun- 
try-side, so blue and tranquil the Adriatic. A re- 
minder of the Orient, however, was given me just 


BUDUA 225 


outside Spizza by a little cavaleade of Turkish 
maidens walking two by two, and bound probably 
for Old Antivari. Over their heads, covering their 
little white wimples, they had blankets to keep the 
rain off them and they wore red Turkish slippers on 
their tiny feet. They passed me quickly, without 
looking, as demure as a ‘crocodile’ on the parade of 
an English watering-place. 

Spizza was a long village straggling round the 
shore of a bay and commanded by hills ending 
in a headland on which was the strong fortress of 
Haj-Nehej, whose guns, much to the chagrin of 
the Montenegrins, completely dominated the Rada 
di Antivari. At the entrance of the little town I 
was confronted by a barricade with a sentry-box, 
striped black and yellow like a wasp, and a soldier 
in his shirtsleeves loafing somewhere near it. He 
looked out of the little guard-room at me as I 
passed, rather inquisitively, but did not take me 
for a spy, in spite of my knapsack. Spizza proved 
to be not an attractive place. There was a little 
sea-front of pink-and-white houses; a cement and 
granite quay or molo which had been so damaged 
by the waves that the passengers by the weekly 
steamer had to be landed in small boats; and, every- 
where, soldiers. The place seemed a mere adjunct 
to a garrison, though it would have been hard to 
discover any seaside ‘resort’ with lovelier surround- 
ings. The interval between the village and the 
mountain slopes was covered with lovely vegeta- 
tion; fig trees, olive groves and beautiful woods; a 
wonderful belt of fertile country with white cot- 
tages and little churches gleaming out here and 
there from among the trees. 


226 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


From Spizza the magnificent highway, oval in 
shape with broad gutters on either side of it to 
drain off the water, began in a series of zigzags to 
climb up over the hill which culminated in the 
fortified headland. Good roads have a fascination 
for me and I went on without delay. The rain beat 
down persistently, yet even this could not conceal 
the beauty of the countryside. I met very few 
people on the way, which was hardly to be won- 
dered at, but when I climbed under the shelter of 
a bridge which carried the road across a splashing 
trout stream, I found, to my surprise, a party of 
about a dozen peasant girls in their pretty native 
costumes, surrounded by a flock of goats. The 
grouping of these girls made one of those vivid 
pictures which remain fixed on the mind’s eye as 
clearly as a work by a great artist. Most of them 
were knitting, and others were sitting doing abso- 
lutely nothing save stare vacantly at the rain. 
When I appeared on the scene, arriving perhaps 
rather unexpectedly round a projecting corner of 
masonry, they were almost too dazed to return my 
salute. They turned, and with one consent gazed 
at me, ‘ox-eyed,’ like so many Heras. 

The situation was certainly a little embarrassing, 
for the costume of an ordinary English young man 
differs in essentials from the free garb of the Aus- 
trian Serb, and attracted amazed consideration. 
Moreover, my. appearance must have been still 
more unusual, for I was soaked to the skin and my 
knapsack was also wet through. This fact, since 
the knapsack contained my sodden luncheon, which 
I now proceeded to eat, seemed to touch some 
chord of proper womanly feeling in one at least of 


BUDUA ei 


the ox-eyed maidens. Very prettily she offered me a 
drink of warm goat’s milk and a piece of cheese, 
which led one to the reflection that really nice 
creatures, in whatever part of the world one meets 
them, bear a remarkable family likeness. 

The beauty of the walk seemed to increase with 
every kilometre, and since leaving England I had 
certainly seen nothing to compare with the loveli- 
ness of this comparatively unexplored region. The 
road was only partially constructed, and for long 
stretches there was only a rough path. Sometimes 
a future bridge would be indicated by a line of thin 
posts, and occasional piles of hewn stones showed 
that work was still spasmodically in progress. 

For the greater part of the way the sea was al- 
ways visible through the trees, and the coast formed 
a succession of the most enchanting coves where the 
vegetation ran down almost to the water’s edge or 
the sea lapped lazily against a wall of rock. The 
country seemed fairly thickly populated, and vines 
abounded, but there were almost as many ruined 
and roofless stone cottages as there were inhab- 
ited ones, and many of the lower slopes of the hills 
bore the remains of ancient forts. One sign of 
Austrian rule (and an excellent sign) was a brand- 
new Government school, white and imposing, from 
whose open windows, as I passed, came the monot- 
onous drone of children reciting their lessons. A 
little way beyond the school the road surmounted 
a low hill, and one saw, below it, a stretch of coun- 
try ending in a broad and peaceful bay, at one end 
of which nestled a little town. It was much farther 
off than it looked, that town; Castel Lastua was its 
pretty name, and it seemed somehow more welcom- 


228 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


ing than Spizza. Two white houses, seaside villas 
perhaps, of local magnates, stood at the entrance to 
the village, which, like Spizza, consisted of a curv- 
ing sea-front, with some narrow streets of primitive 
stone cottages at the back. There were still the 
inevitable soldiers. Intending to get a glass of wine 
and to rest for a moment or two, I stepped into a 
general shop by accident, thinking it to be an inn. 
‘I guess you want to go next door, sare,’ said the 
corpulent proprietor, Mr. Favo Sugyjic. He looked 
like a very cheery Italian organ-grinder, and had 
lived in America, he told me, for twenty years. 
Young olive-cheeked ‘Americans’ were produced, 
who came up and inspected me with the solemn, 
non-committal stare of children. Mr. Sugyjic didn’t 
send me away, but brought me a glass of wine from 
an inner room, and we talked. English-speaking 
villagers seemed to appear magically from the 
street, and we discussed everything from America 
to the Government roads, and the superior virtues 
of Castel Lastua as compared with Spizza. 

It was delightful to come out of the depressing 
rain into shelter and the sound of something ap- 
proximating to one’s own tongue. Mr. Sugyjic told 
me it was an easy walk of some four hours to Budua, 
and that there was a new inn there where I could 
get very tolerable accommodation. He pleased me 
by saying I was the only Englishman he had seen in 
Castel Lastua since his return from America two 
years ago; and sold me an astonishingly bad picture- 
postcard of the cove. A young man of the audience, 
who had also been in America, kindly put me on 
my way, and by climbing up a steep and rocky 
path I picked up the highroad which here rounded 
the hillside en corniche. 


BUDUA 229 


From now onwards there followed the same suc- 
cession of bays and landlocked coves — perfect 
little bays with islands in the midst of them, half 
asleep in a dreaming sea. The slopes of the hills 
were studded with farms, and little white chapels 
with tiny belfries, half-choked with the blue flowers 
which grew also in all the crevices of the walls. 
Here one could look down on a village half hidden 
in trees, by the water’s edge; there one could look 
up at a small white church surrounded by a group 
of cottages, halfway up the hillside. At last I saw 
below me a tiny presqu’ile, joined to the shore by a 
causeway, which was covered by a Venetian walled 
village, containing about fifty or sixty houses 
grouped round a little church. It was a perfect 
architectural jewel, but, alas! it was not my des- 
tination. A girl who scrambled up to the high land 
through the olive groves, bent nearly double under 
a tremendous load, told me the name of it was San 
Stefano. However, I had begun now the last stage 
of my exhausting walk and hadn’t time to explore. 
After crossing one of the many ridges of rising 
ground, I saw below me a wide bay, and on the 
farther side of it, in the distance, the grey walls and 
snowy campanile of Budua. 


II 


It was about five in the afternoon when I first 
looked across that bay and saw Budua in the dis- 
tance — a mysterious line of ancient houses peering 
over an old sea-wall, and with a new gleaming tower 
in the midst of it. I knew very little about the 
place, save the few facts that I had discovered from 
a German guide-book which a friend in the hotel at 


230 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


Cettinje kindly translated for me. From this source 
I learnt that it was built on a low, rocky promon- 
tory, that it was of Phoenician origin and the Butua 
of Pliny; that it had been destroyed by the Saracens 
in the seventh century, then rebuilt by the Vene- 
tians, then in 1571 taken and burnt by the Turks, 
who again in 1687 besieged it with ten thousand 
men under the Pasha of Scutari, on which occasion 
it was gallantly defended by the Venetian Cornaro. 
In modern times it was occupied by the English in 
1798 and by the French in 1814. The number of its 
inhabitants was given as a thousand. Beyond these 
interesting details I knew absolutely nothing about 
the town; I only knew that, come what might, I had 
to spend the night in it. I became filled with excite- 
ment at the thought of the adventures that might 
be in store for me. The rain had stopped now, the 
sky had cleared and the atmosphere had a peculiar 
brightness and transparency which seemed to add 
miles to the eyes’ range, and to give a strange 
glamour to the whole landscape so that all seemed a 
little unreal, a little too good to be true. As I grew 
nearer to Budua it became altogether a dream- 
place, and enchanted — like one of those little 
cities set on a hill that you see through a window, in 
the background of so many Italian paintings. It 
could hardly have looked much different to a 
traveller approaching it on foot three centuries ago 
from its appearance to-day. Its walls were still 
intact and entirely engirdled it, while the only way 
of entering the town was by one of the two gates. 
The larger of these, that leading into the main 
street, was of course protected by the inevitable 
striped sentry-boxes of the K.K. army. The smaller 


BUDUA 231 


gate had no sentry-box, but bore above it the 
winged lion of Saint Marc, gules, surmounting a 
shield with three étoiles on a blue ground. Inside, it 
was protected by a little image of the Virgin, 
stuffed with immortelles, before which a tiny light 
perpetually burned. 

I found my inn just inside the main gate, over 
which its top windows appeared like a man’s head 
over a garden wall, as though to keep an eye on the 
little wooden sailing-vessels lying at anchor under 
the shelter of the molo. To reach my room I had to 
climb an outside staircase, which also gave on to the 
narrow path round the ramparts. This path would 
have enabled one to walk all round the little town 
in five minutes, had not the part of it on the side 
facing the open sea been shut off for the military. 
The house had only been opened a month, and was 
called rather grandiloquently the ‘Hétel Balkan.’ 
It was kept by an apologetic Russian Jew with a 
long nose and that baffling little knowledge of Eng- 
lish which is the most dangerous thing the traveller 
can meet with. He knew just enough to be misin- 
forming. I had arrived dripping, and on reaching 
my room stripped myself and bathed my aching 
limbs as well as I could in the exiguous basin pro- 
vided. Then came the question of clothes. My own 
were a sodden heap. Luckily, it occurred to the 
proprietor’s one-eyed, plump wife to bring me a set 
of her husband’s garments, which included a canvas 
shirt fresh from the laundry, some long drawers 
which tied themselves up with tapes, and a pair of 
boots which, to my extreme mortification, were too 
small for me. I had a great deal of difficulty in ob- 
taining what I wanted, owing to my ignorance of 


232 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


German and Italian. In desperation I suggested 
‘Franzésich,’ which produced an immediate but 
unwelcome effect. I understood that I was to be 
prepared to receive a deputation. Madame, in 
spite of the fact that I was quite sufficiently clad in 
her best eider-down, officiously put a towel over my 
shoulders, and presently ushered in a small girl of 
about twelve who ‘spoke French’ and was an- 
nounced with a flourish of trumpets. Such French! 
Prunes, prisms, Ollendorff, and the quintessence 
of all girls’ schools were contained in its drawing- 
room elegance. Alas! it was limited to inquiries as 
to the way in which I carried myself, and the like. 
Still, it was something to understand even that. 
After she had withdrawn, in the second-best suit of 
the Russian patron, and my own wet boots, I was 
able to get out and explore the town. 

I went first of all on to the ramparts. It was de- 
lightful to spend the hour before the night looking 
over the side of the crumbling wall, luxuriant with 
greenery and with blue flowers growing in its 
crevices, to where the sea lapped its base. 

Looking ahead there was a pretty island with a 
shrine on it, which guarded the entrance to the 
roadstead — its name was San Niccolo — and be- 
yond it were the headland which shut in the bay 
and the lovely hills I had crossed two or three hours 
earlier. 

Looking back, to the left, one saw an amphithea- 
tre of wooded hills, rising to the dark mountains 
of Montenegro, whose higher slopes (but not the 
summits) were draped with filmy white clouds 
which gathered and disappeared like smoke. These 
old ramparts were an enchanting spot for an idler! 


BUDUA 233 


Turning away from the ceaseless interest of watch- 
ing the shadows gathering over the sea and over the 
bay, one could look down into the little back 
gardens of the houses. Riotous plots of greenery 
they were, more luxuriant and surprising even than 
the gardens of Venice; and, as in Venice, the trees 
and climbing plants swarmed unrestrainedly over 
the stone walls, and hung festooning into the narrow 
streets. 

I left the ramparts with reluctance, to explore the 
little town. The area it covered was ludicrously 
small, and most of it was taken up by the fortifica- 
tions, which were then still used to house the so!- 
diers which gave every Dalmatian port the appear- 
ance of a place occupied by a foreign conqueror. 
Most of the streets were hardly more than six feet 
wide, and the palazzi of a vanished nobility towered 
up in a way that would have been impressive if one 
could only have got a proper view of them. Nearly 
all the houses were adorned with coats-of-arms, so 
that one wondered where the common people lived. 
Perhaps after all there was something in the boast 
made by one of the English-speaking inhabitants — 
a splendid old Slav with a head like a lion — who 
came into the hotel after dinner. He said to me 
proudly of himself and his fellow-townsfolk, “We 
are all of the nobility, sir, since Diocletian’s days.’ 

The town had a little central piazza containing a 
church and a good-sized house used as a bank and 
another building which may have been a garrison 
chapel. In the street leading from here to the lesser 
of the two gates was a large and very dark café, in 
which a soldier was sitting with the golden head of 
the waitress pressed against histunic. Hesat like that 


234 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


for about five minutes, holding the head in position 
with a large, rather dirty hand, and bending down 
his lips to the tortoise-shell comb which surmounted 
the top of it. On this spectacle of true love trium- 
phant an old witch of a dame du comptoir peered

few hellers, disengaged itself and brought me my 
beer. Having accomplished this service and pock- 
eted her tip, she resumed her position, and the 
large hand once more pressed her towpet against the 
excited beatings of a military bosom. The twilight 
grew deeper, the silence more intense, the immo- 
bility and indifference of the old woman in the back- 
ground more pronounced. I hastily swallowed my 
drink and fled. 

My next venture was a surprisingly good general 
shop a little farther on, where I was so brilliantly 
charged double for a walking-stick that I became 
convinced the town of Budua had a great com- 
mercial future. I then went out of the town by the 
little gate, protected by Our Lady, and found my- 
self by a gently shelving beach which showed 
Budua to be surrounded by the sea on three sides — 
indeed, to be on a presquile — like San Stefano. I 
walked back, through a grove of carob-trees out- 
side the fortifications, till I came once more to the 
main gate and to my hotel, and found to my cha- 
grin that there was still an hour before dinner, 
which was not till half-past eight.

IIT 


The restaurant of the Hétel Balkan was a big, 
bare, whitewashed room — empty, draughty, and 
without adornment. The tablecloths looked as 
though they remained forlornly on the tables for 
weeks at a time. The few other diners besides my- 
self were huddled together, as if for warmth, at a 
table in the corner, and there was no bill-of-fare. 
One took what one was given. The one-eyed wife 
of the proprietor produced two dishes consisting of 
flat slabs of meat covered in gravy which, when it 
came to the point, were not really as unpalatable as 
they looked. When I had finished eating and had 
arrived, at last, at the delicious coffee one gets in 
any part of Europe where the Turk has ever pene- 
trated, a succession of callers came in to see me, 
headed by the native who spoke English, to whom I 
have referred already. He had been warned of my 
arrival in the town —the news of that exciting 
event must have gone all round the place within an 
hour — and had come to pay his respects and to 
converse with me, while half-a-dozen of his friends 
had accompanied him to watch him doing it. 

He was a fine figure of an old man, immensely tall 
and of the Slav type, with a yellow, furrowed, noble 
face. He wore a bowler on the back of his head, a 
black frock-coat, and a pair of very much shrunken 
white striped flannel trousers. He was a commer- 
cant, he said, with a ‘beautiful’ position at Dul- 
cigno, and was connected by marriage with King 
Nicholas of Montenegro. He told me that Dul- 
cigno was a lovely town, situated on a rocky hill 
overlooking the sea and descending into a small 
bay, about a dozen miles to the southwest of Scu- 


236 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


tari, and that it had seven mosques. I knew at once 
that I should never be perfectly contented until I 
had been to Dulcigno. Alas! it is, I fear, a dream 
city that will now forever elude me. Afterwards, 
we discussed the inevitable ‘situation,’ and the old 
eyes lighted up and caught fire as he told me that 
when the time came he would fight for his com- 
patriots. All the young men of Dalmatia — and 
the old men too — would enrol themselves on the 
outbreak of war in the armies of Servia or Monte- 
negro. He himself would go at once to Cettinje and 
offer his services to King Nicholas. He had volun- 
teered, he said, in the wars of ’63 and ’72. In his 
opinion the death of the old Emperor Francis 
Joseph would bring about the abrupt dismember- 
ment of the Austrian Empire. Hungary would 
break away and become a separate kingdom; Aus- 
tria proper would be absorbed in Germany, and 
the age-long dream of the Slavonic peoples would 
then be fulfilled —the Balkan Confederation. 
Some of the enthusiasm of the patriot dreaming of 
freedom for his people after centuries of subjection 
communicated itself to me. We shook hands with 
what Iam sorry to say was aslightly vinous warmth, 
and I would stake my final shilling that a few 
weeks later, when the Balkan War did break out, 
my friend found a place somewhere in the Monte- 
negrin ranks whence he could fire his rifle at the 
Turk. 

The next day the rain had stopped, and the sun 
shone, but the attentions of one persistent mos- 
quito, whose baleful buzzing and unceasing hunger 
had kept me awake throughout the night, had 
robbed me of all joy in existence. To make things 


BUDUA 237 


worse, there was no seat for me in the diligence, and, 
as the steamer I must catch for home started at 
noon, there was nothing for it but a three to four 
hours’ tramp to Cattaro — ten solid miles —on feet 
the soles of which had already blistered. Fate, 
however, is seldom altogether unkind. She sent me 
on this occasion a Montenegrin girl of about twenty, 
who was also going to Cattaro, and adopted me as 
a travelling companion. She wore the usual round 
Montenegrin cap on her head, a light sleeveless 
woolen coat over her frock, and comfortable sand- 
als. She managed to keep on knitting a pair of 
white stockings as she walked, and made ceaseless 
attempts to get into conversation with me, in spite 
of my apologetic ‘Inglés.’ At a little inn, half-way, 
we drank a cup of wine together, and proceeded on 
our road very cheerful, and hand-in-hand. She had 
bright brown eyes and soft brown hair and a lovely 
brown complexion, and we — or I, at all events — 
parted in Cattaro with more than a twinge of re- 
gret. I could just distinguish her among the crowd 
on the quay as the big steamer slowly moved away 
from her moorings. 

My memories of Montenegro are still vivid after 
an interval of many years since I made the little 
journey here recorded. King Nicholas is dead, and 
his family has been forced to join the ranks of those 
exiled royalties who haunt the French Riviera. 
The Montenegrins, after centuries of liberty, have 
been robbed of their status as an independent 
people and merged, against their wills, in the king- 
dom of Jugo-Slavia. The Great War, that was 
fought by the Allies to defend small nations, has re- 
sulted in the smallest of the nations and the poorest 


238 MONTENEGRIN MEMORIES 


of the Allies being handed over, defenceless, to its 
greedy neighbour. Montenegro has endured a reign 
of terror, but its spirit I can well believe remains 
unbroken. 


THE TRIP TO THE STATES

THE TRIP TO THE STATES 
I 
FIRST IMPRESSIONS 


For a good many generations the trip to the States 
has marked a definite point in the career of the suc- 
cessful English writer, but since the war, almost 
every English man and woman who earns a living 
by writing, whether successful or the reverse, has 
crossed the Atlantic at the invitation of enterpris- 
ing publishers or lecture agents, in the hope of mak- 
ing good. New York has become the principal art 
centre of the world, and the aid of American money 
enables writers and artists in every civilized country 
to pay their bread and butter bills, and carry on 
with their jobs. No wonder we flock to it. 

When, filled with apprehensions, I set foot in the 
New World, for the first time, I found the air full of 
rumours regarding the success or failure of previous 
literary visitors. So-and-so ‘went down awfully 
well’: Such-and-such ‘made a complete flop’: X 
and Y cut no ice at all. As I listened, I seemed to 
see the whole thing in terms of a gymnastic display. 
There was the vaulting-horse, there the long line of 
stripped athletes, awaiting their turns. The compe- 
tition starts. Some clear the horse with the utmost 
ease, amid the plaudits of the onlookers, and are 
borne off to banquets by their backers. Others 
fumble their take-off, fail to get over, and retire, 
blushing with mortification, to hide their dimin- 
ished heads. 

It all seemed very embarrassing, and ‘success’ or 


242 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


‘failure’ seemed to have very little to do with the 
actual literary merits of the competitors, but to de- 
pend upon the tricks of quite another trade. 

Looking back at my own recent experiences, I can 
see that I made almost every kind of initial error. 
To begin with, I travelled cheaply in a big ship, in- 
stead of more cheaply still in a slow ‘cabin class’ 
boat. I was assured that Knights travelled by the 
cheaper boat. On the other hand, Llewellyn Powys 
and Alyse Gregory were going tourist third in the 
Mammoth and my old friends Louis Wilkinson 
and his wife were going down to Southampton to 
see them off, and this, in my innocence, struck me as 
a good enough reason for some additional expendi- 
ture. It was a mistake, of course. If you arrive on 
a slow boat carrying few passengers, the interview- 
ers find you with ease; whereas, if you arrive on a 
monster travelling ‘tourist third’ they search for 
you in vain. Those pearls of wit and wisdom which 
would doubtless have delighted the American pub- 
lic — my first impressions of America — were lost 
irrecoverably. The small posse of pressmen who 
came to look for me never found me. 

I think perhaps I saw more of the real America 
on board the Mammoth than I was destined to see 
on land. The passengers were of every imagina- 
ble origin. There were Czecho-Slovaks, Russians, 
Poles, Alsatians, Italians, Germans, Swedes, Danes, 
Spaniards, Portuguese and Jews, and Jews and 
Jews. Anglo-Saxons could not have constituted as 
many as ten per cent of them. The majority of 
those I encountered found difficulty in speaking 
English, some had hardly a word. But I shall never 
forget, when, on our last evening, the ship’s enter- 


FIRST IMPRESSIONS 243 


tainer, after an informal concert, struck up ‘My 
country ’tis of thee,’ the fervour which all these 
people of such diverse racial types, put into the sing- 
ing of their national anthem. Its words were, I am 
convinced, the only words of English that many of 
them knew. Any expression of genuine emotion is 
impressive, and the emotion here was genuine 
enough. ‘Poor devils,’ one thought, ‘after the 
slums of Central Europe and age-old oppressions 
and degradations, what America must mean to 
them!’ 

The Mammoth did not predispose me in favour 
of American liners. In fact, ever since one of the 
seamen, who omitted to turn up at boat drill through 
mere disinclination to attend, volunteered the in- 
formation that there ‘ain’t no discipline on this 
ship,’ I never felt really safe aboard her. It took 
me three days to get a cablegram sent off; and the 
general slovenliness of the service, the indifferently 
_ cleaned decks, and air of confusion prevailing con- 
trasted with anything I had experienced before of 
ships and their ways. The cabins were unprovided 
with bells, and if you asked a question no one seemed 
to know the answer. The food, however, was excel- 
lent. Any drinking done on board was done in the 
cabins, and I shall never forget the look of shocked 
_ amazement on the steward’s face, at our first 
luncheon on the dock at Southampton, when I asked 
him for a corkscrew in order that Louis Wilkinson 
and I might have a farewell drink together, before 
the ship sailed. A corkscrew was produced at last, 
shamefacedly, and the whisky opened. The feeling 
_ of abject sinfulness and guilt which I had in thus 
openly drinking spirits before the eyes of American 


Q44 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


citizens —even in English waters and within a 
hundred feet of English land — was astonishing. 
When the steward said he wanted to see me after- 
wards, I felt like a schoolboy who has been sum- 
moned ominously to the headmaster’s study. Evi- 
dently it was going to be a ‘dry’ voyage, and there 
was something in this prohibition after all. It was 
dry — outwardly. But I fancy that most of the 
cabins were private speak-easies and that more 
liquor was really consumed on board, in furtive and 
uncomfortable circumstances, than would have 
been consumed in an English ship of the same size. 

I was heartily thankful when the voyage was 
over; and I don’t know when I have set foot on 
land with more relief than I did on my arrival in 
America. After waiting for nearly an hour with my 
fellow-unfortunates, listening to the heartbreaking 
sobs of a wretched woman who had failed to satisfy 
her examiners and was presumably to be sent to 
Ellis Island, I came before the eagle-eyed officer. 
Luckily, up to the present, I have never come within 
the clutches of the Law. But had I actually been 
(a) an anarchist, complete with bomb, arriving with 
the firm intention of overthrowing by force the 
government of the United States, or (6) an active 
and determined bigamist, I do not believe I could 
have felt more guilty than I did during the officer’s 
inquisition. He seemed convinced that I was one of 
these English white trash who creep into the 
United States on false pretences, with no intention 
of ever leaving them. However, with the moral sup- 
port of the kind Powys’ to back me, I was at last al- 
lowed off the ship. 

Heavens, how glad I was! The air was so tonic 


FIRST IMPRESSIONS Q45 


that my spirits rose from deep depression to bound- 
less confidence. I had hardly deposited my luggage 
in my hotel and stepped out into West 44th Street 
before I met Ernest Boyd, looking precisely as he 
looked when I saw him last in Dublin. The brown 
beard, the brown clothes, the genial cynicism, the 
imperturbable independence of outlook, the incor- 
rigible hospitality — all were there, as I remem- 
bered them. 

‘Drop in to-night,’ said Ernest. “Mencken and 
some other people are coming round.’ From that 
moment my memories of New York consist almost 
entirely of parties. As I am one of those misguided 
people who think sociability is an end in itself and 
consistently put pleasure before business, I dis- 
interred from my pockets not those solid letters of 
introduction to important people who ‘might be 
useful,’ with which I was amply supplied, but a 
quantity of cards handed me by boon companions 
in Juan-les-Pins or Montparnasse or in one of those 
secret and mysterious London pubs which have 
their international ramifications, which had this 
sort of message scrawled on the back of them: ‘Dear 
Ted: This is Douglas Goldring. He’s a grand fellow, 
Dick.’ The world, as we all know, is a small place 
and birds of a feather flock together. Those of the 
New York birds I encountered, who belonged to my 
sort of nest — which, alas for me, is not a literary 
one — received me like a long-lost relative. The 
charm of American hospitality has been written 
about too often to need any further description 
here. What I was chiefly conscious of was saying 
over and over to myself: ‘Good Heavens, it’s all 
true. All would have been enchanting but for that 


246 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


menacing gymnastic performance. Sooner or later 
I should have to line up, under the eye of my pub- 
lishers, and enter the competition. While I was en- 
joying myself and putting off the evil day, a youth- 
ful rival with a name sufficiently similar to mine to 
give reviewers and paragraph writers an opportu- 
nity of confusing us, leapt into the limelight. Ina 
word, the electric Louis Golding took the field 
with Hebraic brio, while his blond and Anglo-Saxon 
confrére was dining in speak-easies with his pals, 
still awaiting the signal to start off. 

Perhaps it was fortunate for me that, thanks to the 
interference of a deus ex machina, in the shape of a 
boy bearing a cablegram, the signal never came. A 
family bereavement dragged me hastily back to 
Europe and put all thoughts of ‘ice-cutting’ out of 
my head. Looking back at my hectic weeks in New 
York from the mellow tranquillity of Provence, I 
find it difficult to disinter from my recollections 
much that seems distinctively American, except its 
architecture, and the extraordinary feeling of ex- 
citement and of buoyant confidence which the at- 
mosphere of the place produces. In saying this I 
only confirm what has been said a thousand times 
before. New York undoubtedly makes Europe 
seem depressingly ‘hard-boiled.’ 

Of all the great international centres I have 
visited, New York is the only one in which the 
American tourist is conspicuous by his absence. 
One searches for him in vain. For the rest, I never 
saw a city so indifferent to the minor details of its 
appearance. (The poorest European town would 
have too much civic pride not to do something with 
Washington Square and other open spaces.) I have 


FIRST IMPRESSIONS Q47 


never seen such traffic jams; nor such a vast con- 
sumption of the more potent and noxious forms of 
alcohol; nor such spontaneous friendliness and so- 
ciability; nor such readiness to listen to proposals 
and to judge them on their merits. I was never ina 
town before where the tempo of social life was so 
rapid, nor in one in which the process of pulling 
down and rebuilding was so continuous. And I 
never, in recent years at all events, have felt so re- 
juvenated and so energetic as I did in New York. 
The charm of London, and indeed of Europe, is, I 
suppose largely to be found in that strong ‘sense of 
the past’ which is always bound up with the life of 
the present and dominates our ideas of the roman- 
tic. New York has, to a heady and intoxicating de- 
gree, the ‘sense of the future,’ and I found it ro- 
mantic in a way which I have to admit that I did 
not believe possible. 


II 


THE SPEAK-EASY 
I 


To the ‘jaded European,’ ‘effete Englishman,’ or 
‘poor frog’ nothing is more amusing in the land of 
Prohibition than the means adopted there for ob- 
taining a drink. I remember an old friend once say- 
ing to me reminiscently, regretfully, “Ah, my dear 
boy, Sin was such fun.’ To recapture this Victorian 
delight, which the young have never known and 
the rest of us can but dimly remember, one has only 
to cross the Atlantic. In England, so near has our 
effeteness brought us to the bow-wows that acts 
once regarded as deliciously sinful are now only con- 
sidered unwise, anti-social, bad for the liver and 
kidneys, or merely ‘not done.’ We are even grow- 
ing rapidly soberer, since, owing to the high cost of 
alcohol combined with the ease with which it is ob- 
tainable, only the comparatively rare types who 
really like the stuff now waste their money on it. 
‘Work’ has become, more than ever, ‘the curse of 
the drinking classes,’ for in order to drink consider- 
ably less than our fathers and grandfathers we have 
to work considerably harder. And, as I have ob- 
served, no sinful glamour surrounds our open and 
unfurtive bottle. ‘The English are, indeed, not only 
drinking less but at the same time re-discovering 
their palates. Fine vintages are once more in re- 
quest, port is being restored to its ancient honour- 
able position in academic lives, and a spot of 775 
brandy again triumphs over exotic, stomach-burn- 


THE SPEAK-EASY 249 


ing liqueurs. Despite the flappers and their cock- 
tails, we drink less and less for the ‘kick,’ more and 
more for the education of the palate and the mel- 
lowing of the mind. The vast potations of that (for 
his period) moderate and abstemious country par- 
son, the Reverend Samuel Woodforde, even the al- 
coholic breakfasts of Ouida’s guardsmen, have for 
ever passed away. In regard to drinking, it is only 
when our American friends come over to our side 
and press upon us whiskies-and-sodas at breakfast 
that we in Europe ever get the sinful thrill of doing 
what is wrong, unwise and rather fun. In America, 
of course, we effete ones get this thrill again and yet 
again, and all the time. Perhaps even that noble 
and truly inspiring and persuasive figure, Miss 
Maude Royden, got an added zest for an occa- 
sional cigarette from her recent visit to Chicago. 
All I can say is that, in every meaning the words 
can convey, I for my part never knew what whisky 
could taste like till I went to a speak-easy. 

I was not wholly unprepared. Just before I sailed 
for New York a friendly American Boy in London 
handed me a soiled card with an address on it, say- 
ing as he did so, ‘I’msorry, but this is about all I can 
do for you.’ I did not make any use of that particu- 
lar card, but I had not been two days in New York 
before I was introduced to and, as it were, made a 
member of two or three enchanting ‘haunts of vice.’ 
‘Vice,’ I suppose, is anything which the majority of 
a community decide to consider as such. To smoke 
cigarettes may be a vice, to eat beef, to eat pork, to 
hold the view that the world is not flat but orange- 
shaped, or generally speaking to indulge appetites 
in ways which are not usual in the community of 


250 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


which you happen to form a part, or to express 
opinions not generally held at some particular time 
and place. Vices have obviously much in common 
with virtues, and the hardened traveller, bewildered 
by the many different conceptions of commonplace 
acts held in widely separated climes, must often be 
at a loss to tell t’other from which, and be forced 
back indeed on the Greek counsel of perfection, 
undev dyav — ‘nothing too much’’—as summing 
up, conveniently, the whole business. 

Speak-easies, I am told, exist in incredible num- 
bers all over New York, and cater for all classes and 
all purses. I went once to a homely Italian restau- 
rant in Greenwich Village, where I had a cocktail 
made of furniture polish, a bottle of harmless home- 
made red wine, and a simple, well-cooked Italian 
meal. Without the furniture-polish I might easily 
have been in Soho. On several other occasions I 
lunched or dined in the Washington Square dis- 
trict in quite elaborate restaurants, where the cook- 
ing was admirable, the white Chianti completely 
convincing, and the liqueur with the demi-tasse 
reasonably drinkable. But the speak-easy I best re- 
member was located in a respectable 1860 town 
house in the ‘forties.’ You went in, with your in- 
troducer, after a stern preliminary inspection, by a 
basement door, or rather gate. The iron gate un- 
locked, you found yourself in a narrow passage, 
with a small bar parlour on the right. After imbib- 
ing a cocktail or two (in this case, as far as I could 
judge, the liquor was wholly authentic), you 
emerged from below-stairs and entered the restau- 
rant for lunch or dinner. The decorations of the 
room were in a curious blend of the ‘mid-Victorian’ 


THE SPEAK-EASY 251 


and the Louis Philippe styles; the decorum and re- 
spectability would have been almost oppressive but 
for the joy of the forbidden, the glamour of the 
‘sinful haunt.’ My knowledge of the Mavson 
Tolérée happens to be limited — but yes, my mind 
flew back twenty years to a tremulous hour spent 
in Marseilles in just such surroundings of dingy, 
fly-blown splendour. ‘Sin’ and plush settees must 
have a subtle connecting link. (‘Sin’ also, I may ob- 
serve, costs the earth, wherever you may choose to 
indulge in it.) The particular speak-easy which I 
adopted as my own was at one time much fre- 
quented by an Anglo-American friend, of hospit- 
able habits. When, on my first visit, I asked the 
genial Italian proprietor if he remembered Captain 
Alexander P , a blend of consternation and re- 
spect showed itself at once upon his expressive 
countenance. ‘My God,’ he stammered, ‘shall I 
ever forget him!’ So I had the enirée, all right. It 
was like walking in, as a new member, to a rather 
terrifying club, arm-in-arm with the Prince of 
Wales. Incidentally, the best restaurant food I 
tasted in New York was in this establishment. Or 
perhaps it only seemed the best, since, as we all 
know, ‘a congenial atmosphere’ has so marked an 
effect upon the happy flowing of the gastric juices. 
Just as ‘vegetarian’ restaurants are almost invari- 
ably incapable of cooking vegetables palatably, so 
the Prohibition restaurant, even if one is on the 
wagon of one’s own free-will, lacks (for me, at all 
events) an indefinable something which robs its 
food of interest. I don’t pretend to know why this 
is. Alcohol may be as noxious a poison as certain of 
the rich solids with which, with one’s knife and fork, 


252 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


one daily digs one’s unavoidable grave. It may 
even be worse. But, taking the average experience 
of the human race throughout the ages, it is surely 
associated far less with crime, punishment, igno- 
minious death, poverty, cruelty and vice than it is 
with good-fellowship, kindness, fine scholarship, the 
arts of music, painting, literature and criticism, 
and, above all, conversation. It is an impartial 
liberator and solvent. For if it liberates what is evil 
in the evil, it also liberates all the good qualities of 
decent people. However, wiser men than I must 
argue the matter, if indeed it needs to be argued. 
All I know is that the pleasantest and most illumin- 
ating conversations I had in New York were in cir- 
cumstances in which the Eighteenth Amendment to 
the Constitution might never have existed. 


II 


Of all the great cities in which I have so far set a 
rather nervous foot, New York is far and away the 
best for talk. Any Englishman who takes an inter- 
est in men and affairs, in the arts, in politics and in 
life, must experience an enormous sense of relief on 
arriving in Manhattan. The frightful discipline of 
English social life which bars as topics of conversa- 
tion any subject that is in the slightest degree worth 
talking about, seems mercifully not to have crossed 
the Atlantic. In New York you can exchange ideas 
on world-politics, religions, literature, the future of 
marriage, the war and its ethics, Anglo-American 
relations — indeed, anything under the sun. And 
the more ideas of your own you possess, even if they 
are considered unusual or wrong, the more eagerly 
you are listened to. I found, during my stay, no 


THE SPEAK-EASY 253 


trace of the rigid English upper and middle-class 
attitude that ‘our mind is made up, and we do not 
wish to discuss the matter further.’ The process of 
gently slamming the door upon all themes of intel- 
lectual interest, by which the English hostess pre- 
serves the secret of her own inadequacy while main- 
taining her prestige in the eyes of her guests, is, I 
believe, unknown in New York society. (I am now 
speaking, of course, chiefly from hearsay. My own 
very limited experience, however, fully bore out 
what other Englishmen with wider knowledge have 
told me.) At number 163 — I must not mention, of 
course, the number of the street — Ted, Jack, 
Lillian and myself, fortified by a few cocktails, used 
to spend hour after hour in eager discussion. They 
were not, of course, ‘typical Americans.’ Indeed, 
of all the hundreds of Americans I have met in 
America and in Europe, I have encountered very 
few that are. What is a ‘typical American,’ any- 
way? Perhaps he does not yet exist. Perhaps he is 
Babbitt? In New York and in Boston I never came 
across any one who had what I always fondly 
imagined to be an ‘American accent.’ The intona- 
tion was agreeable, different from ours, o’s were 
broadened into a’s, and ‘yeah’ or ‘yep’ took the 
place of ‘yes.’ Otherwise it was just good, nervous, 
educated English, strengthened by fresh and vivid 
turns of phrase. ‘Wall, I guess,’ and ‘Say, stranger,’ 
are, perhaps, only a convention of the English 
music-halls. Anyhow, I never heard them once. 
Ted, on my first evening, was particularly anxious 
for me to tell him what I thought was the average 
English point of view about America. At this I 
jibbed. ‘I'll talk the donkey’s hind-leg off, if you 


Q54 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


like, about one Englishman’s point of view,’ I said. 
‘But what the average Englishman thinks, unless I 
am he, how should I know?’ 

“Well, just go on and tell us a bit,’ urged Lillian. 
“We do really want to sense your viewpoint.’ I 
again expostulated at this, to me, embarrassing re- 
quest. But I remembered the aplomb with which 
numbers of my fellow countrymen of no more than 
average intelligence yearly lectured large audiences 
throughout the States about the Americans and 
their country, and took heart. I then remembered 
the many sub-acid comments I had heard in New 
York about these same ‘superior’ Englishmen, and 
lost heart. Anyway, it seemed rough to come toa 
country for the first time, to meet old friends and 
perhaps to make new ones, and to be asked at once 
to express an opinion of some sort about one’s hosts. 
An Englishman or a Frenchman never asks an 
American to his face what he thinks of England or 
of France, not because he doesn’t care to know, but 
because, with social foresight, he sees the dilemma. 
Either the visitor will say what he thinks, with em- 
barrassing candour; or else he will say what he thinks 
will please, and that also will be embarrassing. 

‘The awful thing is, Lillian,’ I said, ‘that I don’t 
believe most of us think about America at all. We 
just take her for granted. Nearly all our press is 
pro-American, most of our journalists seem to make 
a point always of understating the English case in 
controversial matters between the two countries, so 
that your traditional popularity in England re- 
mains unimpaired, despite occasional sorenesses. 
Some of us wish you wouldn’t do so much propa- 
ganda in Europe through your films, especially your 


THE SPEAK-~EASY 255. 


war films; and a great many of us would like 
America to stay as American as possible and allow 
us to remain English. London is rapidly becoming 
Americanized —architecture, music, speech, man- 
ners, morals. We badly need a British “‘Big Bill 
Thompson” to counteract the insidious American 
attempts to undermine our national character, 
change our habits and force prohibition on us. 
Generally speaking, there never was a time when 
America had such a vogue in Great Britain as she 
has to-day. American music, American negro sing- 
ers, American literature, American plays, American 
architecture, American cocktails, American busi- 
ness methods, even American slang — they are all 
the rage with us at present. Hardly any one, except 
politicians and leader-writers, wants to be bothered 
with such questions as the debt settlements, the 
League of Nations, your occasional outbursts of 
sabre-rattling imperialism, and all the rest of it. 
When we see scare headlines like ““ War with Eng- 
land inevitable — says Mayor of Bingeville,”’ we 
just buy a few more American records, put them on 
our American gramophone, and start an American 
jazz.’ 

‘Bootleg gin, that war talk,’ said Jack. ‘Means 
nothing.’ 

‘Quite so. As we see it, no war was ever less in- 
evitable or more unlikely. We realize America’s 
financial world-domination and don’t underesti- 
mate her strength. But we are not in the least 
afraid of her and we don’t really envy her — so the 
principal causes of war are at once ruled out. What 
the average Englishman likes more than wealth or 
bathrooms is personal liberty. If he can’t get 


256 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


enough of that in his own country, he goes where he 
can. France, incidentally, is about the freest coun- 
try in the world to-day. America seems to us, from 
what we can hear, one of the least free. I suppose 
both our countries think each other vicious and im- 
moral. Your crime statistics, political scandals, and 
judicial procedure shock us profoundly. Certainly 
the solid English bourgeoisie thinks that America, 
by starting the fashion for easy and frequent di- 
vorces, has undermined that sacred Victorian insti- 
tution, the British middle-class home, respectable 
and enduring. What the rest of us can’t understand 
is, not why you divorce, but why you marry such a 
lot.’ 

‘Ah, that’s the New England conscience,’ said 
Ted. ‘It governs our “necking” parties, too. 
Provides the necessary kick, the spice of danger. 
Thus far and no further! Besides, the women insist 
on the ceremony. They know that marriage makes 
cowards of us all. And, of course, in the land of the 
Puritans, they like to be respectable!’ 

Lillian didn’t listen to these trivialities. She was 
earnest and well-informed and scented in me, de- 
spite my reluctance to hold forth, a political mis- 
sionary retired from business. She nosed about like 
a foxhound to get on the track of my ‘viewpoint.’ 
But the volcano was extinct. No ‘great words 
winged to-day’ were going to be winged by me. I 
felt warm and cosy on my plush seat. ‘All civilized 
people belong to two countries, Lillian dear,’ I re- 
marked, in what was intended to be a big bow-wow 
style, ‘— their own, and that fatherland without 
frontiers which is, well, the country of civilized 
people. Ententes cordiales between nations, engi- 


THE SPEAK-EASY 257 


neered by newspaper propaganda, “hands-across- 
the-sea”’ leagues and so forth, are as nauseating to 
civilized human beings as hatreds between nations, 
produced and engineered by similar means. As true 
civilization increases...’ 

“Ah, I thought so,’ said Lillian. ‘ You’re really an 
internationalist.’ 

Caught at last, the pin transfixing me, I was 
ticketed, labelled and put away in Lillian’s butter- 
fly-case. Would I speak on Friday week? I would 
not. I would only speak-easy. 


ITI 


My encounters with Ford Madox Ford, with 
Ernest and Madeleine Boyd, and other confréres 
from across the Atlantic had opened my eyes to the 
fact that New York, which one is assured so per- 
sistently is not America, is a great international 
clearing-house in which, at any moment, one may 
meet anybody he has ever known. As a centre of 
civilization, of all the civilizations, I am not sure 
that it does not put Paris and London completely 
in the shade. I should not like to live in it, however, 
for the simple reason that I should not live. I im- 
agine that six months would about see the end of 
me — but what a six months! I soon got used to 
running across people I hadn’t met for years, and 
after my encounter with Dick at Number 163, I be- 
came inured to surprises. Number 163, like Jim- 
my’s in Montparnasse or ‘Mr. K’s’ in London, was 
evidently the sort of place where anybody belong- 
ing to my small world might be expected to turn up. 

I had not seen Dick for nearly fifteen years, and 
I confess that if he had not hailed me I should not 


258 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


have recognized him. His erstwhile rather decadent 
fragility was now concealed by a pointed beard and 
his clothes no longer had those ‘ Lord Lillian’ graces 
which had made his appearance notable in 1907. 
Like his books, he had mellowed. ‘It’s a funny 
thing,’ Dick remarked, when we had greeted one 
another, ‘but I’ve just been talking to a chap who 
knew James Flecker, and all the crowd who used to 
gather in Isabel Davidson’s flat. I couldn’t place 
him, but he seemed to have known us all. Do you 
remember that absurd tea-party at Isabel’s when 
we solemnly read out our little poems and you dis- 
graced yourself by bursting into giggles when Jack 
Beazley recited one of his most serious efforts? I 
think Trelawney was there, if I remember right, 
and Fremantle.’ I recalled the scene vividly. We 
were a collection of young poets trying rather con- 
sciously to create a Parisian atmosphere in a South 
London flat, and I can’t think why I giggled, unless 
it was because we were all so absurdly solemn. 
The verse which occasioned my unfortunate lapse 
has remained in my memory ever since, and I 
quoted it to Dick: 


Christ Jesus for the sorrowing tribe of men 

Two thousand years gone died the death of shame; 
Rose from the grave, but since is dead again. 
Therefore what boots it calling on His name? 

Now must man aidless bear his joy or graeme; 

Since curse nor prayer the steep-up Heaven can climb, 
“Take every man his turne, as for his time.’ 


‘I always thought “the young but learned 
friend”’ a better poet than James,’ said Dick. ‘Do 
you remember his poem, “The Visit,” that you 
printed in your magazine, “The Tramp,” with 


THE SPEAK-EASY 259 


Trelawney Dayrell-Reed’s accompanying picture? 
And the “Cleopatras”’ and “ Vanavitra’’?’ 

“No doubt he was more accomplished than 
Flecker was in 1907 and 1908,’ I replied. ‘But 
Flecker developed rapidly in the next few years, 
while the “learned friend”’ resumed his archeology 
and, as far as we know, forsook the Muse. Think of 
the difference between Flecker’s first book, “‘ The 
Bridge of Fire,” and the little book I printed for 
him in “The Tramp” days — “ Thirty-Six Poems.” 
And the difference between that and “‘The Golden 
Journey to Samarkand.” Flecker was a single- 
minded, hard-working poet. Poetry was his job. I 
only met Beazley a few times, but it struck me that 
writing verse was just a diversion in the midst of 
what, to him, were more serious pursuits. At a 
guess, I should say he had too fine a critical mind to 
take himself seriously as a poet, and not enough 
emotion or native impulse to force him, like A. E. 
Housman and T. S. Eliot, to produce and publish 
in spite of a barrage of self-criticism. I don’t know. 
Somehow I have always thought that, through 
Beazley’s self-imposed silence, that period probably 
lost one of its biggest men.’ 

“Beazley produced the samples,’ Dick observed, 
in his tottery, quavering falsetto, ‘but, after all, 
Flecker delivered the goods. The older I grow, the 
more conservative I become in my literary tastes. 
I like something solid for my money. Shakespeare 
and Edgar Wallace, you know; they never let one 
down.’ 

We were joined, at this moment, by two young 
‘cerebrals, very fresh from Oxford. They were 
about the same age that Dick and I were, when 


260 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


Flecker and Beazley — ‘James and John, the sons 
of Zebedee’ — were the twin stars of our circle. 
Curiously we observed them across the gulf of the 
intervening generation. They seemed extremely 
bored, but full of assurance, and their conversation 
was erudite and brilliant. Their ‘reaction’ to 
America struck the new note — that is to say, the 
note struck by Oscar Wilde in the nineties and 
banged again by Wyndham Lewis and C. R. W. 
Nevinson and others since. Apart from the charac-: 
teristic beauties of New York architecture, a sub- 
ject worn threadbare by this time, they were deli- 
cately enraptured by factories, power-stations, gas- 
works, the zsthetic beauty of industrial plants. 
After an overdose of Italy, New York was proving 
‘so stimulating.’ 

‘There’s not much simple gusto about these 
boys,’ Dick observed, as we withdrew into a corner 
of the bar. ‘But they are the genuine article all 
right. In our young days, as “‘decadongs”’ we were 
a bit of a fraud. We lapped up Trelawney’s poems, 
particularly the one beginning ‘I am so tired of all 
that I remember, So tired of everything that I for- 
get,’ with youthful zest. But it was a pose with us. 
These young chaps really are born disillusioned and 
wearily sceptical.’ 

‘But they nearly all of them write extraordinarily 
well,’ I said. ‘And they know such a damned lot. 
They appear to have read everything in all litera- 
tures, to have seen every picture, every play, every 
dancer...’ 

‘That’s their trouble,’ Dick rejoined. ‘They 
can’t help coruscating. And, toa man, they “freely 
and sportively bombinate.” No “cerebral”’ can 


THE SPEAK-EASY 261 


ever help writing rather well, particularly about 
other people’s writings. You couldn’t imagine T. S. 
Eliot, who is probably the best of the lot, or even 
one of the lesser lights, like David Garnett, ever 
writing really badly. Contrast the fantastic wallow- 
ings of a Shelley or a Keats! Would any verse- 
writer of to-day dare to print anything as bad asa 
really bad Keats? On the other hand, just look at 
the work of some of our best living writers — Ford 
Madox Ford, D. H. Lawrence, H. G. Wells, Violet 
Hunt, Ezra Pound, for example. They can all give 
you that cold-shiver-down-the-back feeling, when 
they like. But when they don’t like! Catch Ford in 
a worried and contracted-for moment, when the 
Spirit isn’t in him. Wrestle with D. H. Lawrence 
when he’s got his nasty-temper-plus-inferiority- 
complex mood on him, and decides that all the 
world’s a lump of dirt, except himself and a few 
Indians! Try Violet Hunt, when she falls off her 
best near-Bronté level. See how you have to 
clamber about amongst Wells’ vast output, before 
you can dig out the solid chunks of genius. You can 
nearly always tell genius by its lapses. I love W. B. 
Yeats’ story of finding Synge in Paris, busy writing 
articles on French poetry, which he couldn’t get 
printed simply because they were so bad. Can you 
imagine any of the younger generation of literary 
*‘cerebrals’’ ever turning out a really bad article, 
or ever writing a really first-rate novel, play or 
poem?’ 

This struck me as being a great deal too sweeping, 
and I said as much. ‘Of course, a trained literary 
journalist becomes efficient, like any other profes- 
sional man. And plenty of excellent writers are also 


262 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


trained journalists. Aldous Huxley is a case in 
point. But you can’t lump him amongst your 
*“cerebrals.’’ From that point of view he is ruined. 
Sentiment and humanitarianism find their way into 
nearly everything he writes. Hedisplaysall kinds of 
common, red-blood “feelings’’ — anger, pity, sym- 
pathy, and all the rest of it. Why, at the end of one 
of his novels, ““Those Barren Leaves,” I think, he 
made the hero watch the Italian sunset reflected 
rosily on the mountains, and come to the conclu- 
sion, staggering to a grandson of the great Thomas 
Henry, that perhaps, after all, there might be a 
God.’ 

‘Arnold will out,’ said Dick, slily. 

‘I don’t care,’ I rejoined. ‘When you can find a 
*‘cerebral’’ who can feel the shuddering hurts and 
horrors that push their heads up in “‘ Antic Hay,” 
there’ll be a chance for him. If Huxley is too ac- 
complished to write badly, if he can’t turn out a 
““middle”’ that isn’t “‘delicious,”’ at all events he’s 
one of the few, out of the whole bunch of polished 
writers, who has a chance of reaching the “cold- 
shiver”’ level.’ 

‘There’s no passion in modern literature,’ Dick 
quavered. ‘No passion! It’s all so cold and clever 
and unrepressed. The mid-Victorian period, that 
was the period when timely utterances relieved the 
chaste voleano. There is infinitely more passion in 
Christina Rossetti and Emily Bronté than in all 
the “‘ Last night, ah, yesternights”’ that ever were 
written by the sloppily dissolute. To get anywhere 
with the physical it has to be taken to worlds be- 
yond the power of bishops to imagine. If you 
plunge right through pornography you come out the 


THE SPEAK-EASY 263 


other end, into a clear polar air of pagan mysticism; 
beyond good and evil, in short. No one can do it, 
not even Joyce, although the icy austerity of 
*“Ulysses”’ illustrates my point.’ 

“It’s queer what a vogue that difficult and im- 
pressive literary eruption has had among the type 
of people whom Ernest Boyd once brilliantly de- 
scribed as “the chemically pure,”’’ I observed. 
* What on earth can the dear, white-haired spinsters 
with horn-rimmed glasses, who have bought ten 
profitable editions of it, make of the thing — be- 
yond getting a thrill from the improper words? 
What do they know about Clongowes and Rath- 
mines, about Irish Catholic pudicity? How on 
earth can they understand those pages and pages of 
condensed Dublin slang? To one who has never 
walked past a Dublin pub when the door suddenly 
opens, and a flood of talk, bad air, tobacco-smoke 
and whisky-fumes pours out on him, I can’t see 
how much of “‘Ulysses’”’ can have any meaning. 
Personally I take Mr. Valéry Larbaud with a pinch 
of salt. Half the book must be incomprehensible, 
even to Englishmen who have not lived in Ireland, 
and still more so to Frenchmen and Americans. 
Why, it is, in essence, the reaction of a poor little 
Irish Catholic boy, educated at a Jesuit school, who 
wanted to “leave the room” and didn’t dare say so. 
Now he has got his own back and a good bit more. 
The explosion has produced a work of genius. Two 
works of genius, if you include “‘The Portrait of the 
Artist as a Young Man.” But I doubt if half their 
admirers can make head or tail of them; and of the 
professional critics who have turned out so many 
hundreds of columns about Joyce, Boyd seems to 


264 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


me to be the only one among them all who has the 
necessary qualifications to form a judgment.’ 

‘What about Ezra?’ Dick objected. 

‘Pound is hors concours,’ I said. ‘He has a flair. 
It’s part of his gift. And he knows his man, if he 
doesn’t know Ireland. Pound, like Ford, has a pure, 
passionate, disinterested love of good writing. He 
is one of the few men living who have a sense of 
team-work. So long as good literature continues 
to be produced, it doesn’t so much matter to him 
who produces it, or where it crops up. When I used 
to see a good deal of him, 1t seemed to me that he 
camouflaged extraordinary generosity of outlook in 
regard to other writers under a mask of rather 
amusing self-assertion. As a literary foster-mother 
Ezra has had few equals in our time. Let us look 
towards Rapallo and drink his health!’ 

Dick and I consumed two dollars’ worth of rye 
whisky in Ezra Pound’s honour and afterwards 
found ourselves included in a party. Our objective 
was the “Sugar-Cane”’ in Harlem, but we stopped 
en route. At the flat we visited first another party 
was in progress which enveloped us, incorporated 
us and bore us off to a third and still larger party, 
where champagne was flowing inexhaustibly and a 
negro orchestra was working up the excitement. At 
about five in the morning, standing on Riverside 
Drive under the pale stars, I decided that Harlem 
must be postponed. Within a few hours the tele- 
phone would ring. Some kind friend had asked me 
to breakfast and proposed to take me to the top of 
the Woolworth Building — me, who cannot look 
out of a fourth-floor window without an internal 
shudder! Or perhaps it was to see Wall Street, and 


THE SPEAK-EASY 265 


Big Business in action. In any case, the hour of my 
appointment was imminent. I did not feel tired, I 
never did in New York, but I told myself that I 
was now past the age when I could stay up with im- 
punity till the hour when the birds begin to sing. 
We caught a lemon-yellow taxi with a negro chauf- 
feur, and as we darted through the night beneath 
the towering buildings, Dick began in his high, tot- 
tering voice to tell me his impressions of the Ameri- 
cans. ‘They are beautifully young, my dear Doug- 
las,’ he quavered, ‘beautifully young! The vernal 
charm of their old men, absorbed in trivialities like 
Oil Trusts or gigantic motor combines, is simply 
touching. It is good for the world to have one na- 
tion at least and a new one, which takes things at 
their face value. Pop buys a “dook”’ for Sadie be- 
cause she wants to feel swell; and a genuine histori- 
cal medieval castle, for himself, to re-erect on 
Long Island. Wonderful to be able to believe that 
the biggest thing must always be the best, the most 
costly the most desirable! When the goal is so clear, 
so simple, no wonder they make straight for it, 
while we others hesitate and are lost. The “big” 
gesture, the “big” idea, the “big”’ success, the “‘big”’ 
heart, too. They have that. Sometimes I wake in 
the night and whisper to myself the blessed words 
“first national’? and ‘“‘demi-tasse,” “‘toilet’? and 
“elevator,” “block” and “‘cafeteria.’”? Mysterious, 
evocative words, they give me glimpses of a poem 
which I shall never be able to write. The place- 
names of America are often poems in themselves — 
Manhattan, Nantucket, Oklahoma, Ohio, Ken- 
tucky, Salem. How I love them. And the “‘college 
boys” and the “‘co-eds”’! No one who has not seen 


266 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


Americans in America can realize their irresistible 
charm. They go rushing on and take the European 
visitor with them as long as he can stand the pace. 
But where are they rushing to, my dear Doug- 
las? Progress... whither and to what? If we only 
knew, if they only knew, their destination, we might 
have a glimpse of the future history of the world for 
the rest of this century. Obviously they cannot 
proceed at such a rate without getting somewhere 
...or running off the rails. I know swarms of 
American “‘idealists.”’ Pacifists, thank God, abound 
in the States and are immensely powerful. Much 
recent American literature is magnificent, and they 
can not only produce such books as “‘The Bridge of 
San Luis Rey” and “The Cabala,”’ but they have a 
public which will buy and read them. But they also 
pass laws to make men virtuous, virtuous according 
to the peculiar standards of horrific ladies of bound- 
less energy, with eyes like those of a hanging judge. 
Thumbs down for you and me, my dear! What Mrs. 
Lavinia K. Bloggs, President of the First National 
Council for Male Reform, of Big Rock, Tennessee, 
Says, goes.’ 

‘But, Dick,’ I said, ‘If Oscar was a wily man, 
Satan is wilier still. What Lavinia says goes. But 
not very far ...even in God’s Own Country. Not 
as far as Number 163, anyway. Here is my barrack. 
Let’s meet before lunch at our “Speak-Easy,”’ and

‘A bunch, my dear boy,’ quavered Dick, ‘of 
chaps.’ 

A bunch it was.
