BOSTON 
I 

‘So you're going to Boston?’ said Peter. ‘You'll 
find it colder, more austere, more of a piece, less 
cosmopolitan than New York. Watch out for the 
New England conscience though, and dust up your 
culture. Brows are high in Boston. But you'll 
probably enjoy yourself. Ring me up when you 
return.’ 

We finished our rye cocktails and I went along to 
the Algenquin to pack. L., tall as a lamp-post and 
slightly bent back as to the head and shoulders, like 
a tree growing on a wind-swept shore, was waiting 
for me at the hotel. For the past few days he had 
looked after me like a sad but kindly nurse. In his 
company I felt frivolous, irresponsible, insufficiently 
grown-up and rather inclined to be naughty; and 
such odds and ends of culture as I have managed to 
acquire in the course of a life-time got mislaid. 

We entered a vast station, the Grand Central, 
and I felt like an ant crawling across the stone floor 
of a cathedral. Luckily, thousands of other ants 
were doing the same, so I did not suffer from agora- 
phobia. In the wake of our negro porter we left the 
nave of the cathedral and entered a dark tunnel 
where the train awaited us. Our reservations, 
mercifully for me, were in a smoking-car, appar- 
ently the only one there was. I gathered that in 
American trains smoking, while not prohibited, 
was not considered quite. .. . Chewing, on the other 


268 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


hand.... We relapsed into our magazines as the 
train swept through a melancholy autumn land- 
scape dotted with suburban houses, ‘gas’ pumps 
and manufacturing plants. Every now and then an 
agglomeration of these, bisected by a brightly ht 
main street, indicated a town. As I peered through 
the window I found myself wondering whether life 
in one of these places really was a fuller, more 
satisfactory affair than life in the mellowed cities of 
‘worn-out’ Europe. Perhaps these ‘burgs,’ on 
acquaintance would prove as disappointing as the 
train which bore me past them, which was certainly 
inferior in smoothness and comfort to most English 
express trains. 

The next morning I woke early, consumed my 
iced grapefruit, and set out to explore Boston. 
It was a cold, clear, sunny autumn day, with white 
clouds drifting about a pale blue sky. I walked to 
the top of the street, stood on the steps of the impos- 
ing State House, and gazed across the far-stretch- 
ing Common. On my right descended a delightful 
street of fine eighteenth-century houses which sur- 
veyed the Common as Piccadilly surveys Green 
Park. Some vandal in the seventies or eighties of 
the last century has destroyed its ensemble by 
erecting an eyesore several stories higher than the 
other houses. Nevertheless Beacon Hill has pre- . 
served more of its original character than Picca- 
dilly. Behind it I found a network of streets, a 
whole quarter, of eighteenth-century brick houses, 
intact and unspoiled. But for the fact that the 
houses were obviously well cared for by their own- 
ers, these streets would have reminded me of parts 
of Dublin. Sometimes, through open windows, I 


BOSTON 269 


caught glimpses of stately Colonial furniture and 
mellow family portraits similar to those examples 
of early American art which I had been admiring a 
few days before in the Metropolitan Art Gallery at 
New York. I had not read my Hergesheimer for 
nothing, and I found myself becoming as senti- 
mental about Boston as any American tourist might 
be about the London of Charles Dickens. 

After I had called on my publishers, L. noting 
my ‘colonial’ enthusiasms, carried me off on a 
sight-seeing expedition. Overshadowed by cliff- 
like commercial buildings in the centre of the city 
was the charming Old State House of brown brick, 
still adorned on the outside with the Lion and the 
Unicorn, which especially aroused my interest. 
With the help of this building, of Beacon Hill, and 
of one or two old churches, one could get some sort 
of idea of what eighteenth-century Boston must 
have looked like. The Boston of a century earlier 
was epitomized for me in a little two-storied seven- 
teenth-century wooden cottage, Paul Revere’s 
house, which has somehow survived the period of 
expansion and is now piously preserved. But of all 
the historic monuments which L. showed me, the 
one from which I got the biggest thrill was an early 
eighteenth-century church with a high steeple and 
old-fashioned box pews —called, I think, the 
North Church — that was crowded with memories 
of Washington and the War of Independence and 
filled with an unseen congregation of staunch New 
England ghosts. By shutting the eyes one could 
transport one’s self in imagination to the great days 
of the ‘Boston tea-party,’ when the English settlers 
in America showed how well they had preserved the 
essential characteristics of their race. 


270 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


L. led me up to an old churchyard overlooking 
the harbour, and together we meditated among the 
graves of the fathers and mothers of New England 
and deciphered the touching inscriptions on their 
tombstones. It was queer to reflect that though 
these grim old rebels were men of sturdy English 
stock, they had become, ina century and a half, the 
national heroes of tens of thousands of little Argen- 
tines, Portuguese and Greeks of American birth, 
not to mention millions of Irish, Italians, and Jews. 
And all because they had successfully defied King 
George III, who was, after all, a German. No 
wonder many Americans are touchy about racial 
origins and insistent upon being one hundred per 
cent American. I have heard George Washington 
spoken of as if he had no earthly origin whatever, 
but appeared miraculously on the American conti- 
nent, the legendary founder of a new people. The 
fact that most Englishmen continue to regard 
Americans as ‘cousins’ and the United States as 
predominantly an Anglo-Saxon country — in other 
words, to think of America in ‘colonial’ terms — 
must be galling to average American opinion. If 
our statesmen could have put the idea of ‘cousin- 
ship’ out of their minds and regarded the United 
States as a completely ‘foreign’ country, formed 
out of a hotch-potch of European races, many of 
which have a deep-rooted hostility to England, and 
an instinctive dislike of Anglo-Saxon civilization 
and habits of thought, one feels that they might 
have avoided some of the errors of policy which have 
endangered Anglo-American amity. During the 
short time that I was in America I was made to feel 
over and over again that England’s persistent woo- 


BOSTON 271 


ing of America’s friendship, the fulsome generosity 
of Mr. Baldwin’s debt settlement, and the rather 
grovelling attitude of the English press whenever 
controversial matters arise between the two coun- 
tries, have been misinterpreted and have to a large 
extent defeated their own object. 

As we walked along briskly towards our lunch — 
Mr. G. had invited me to the Saint Botolph Club 
to meet Conrad Aiken, Mr. Samuel Merwin, and 
Professor Peterson, the author of the new ‘Life’ of 
Havelock Ellis —I am afraid L. must have found 
me silent and abstracted. I couldn’t very well ex- 
plain to him that though I am now, politically 
speaking, a comfortably extinct volcano, an in- 
dividualist relapsed into engrained Toryism, I still 
occasionally allow myself secret orgies of rumina- 
tion on ‘world’ topics. There was an exalted and 
happy period of my life when I was a fanatical, 
single-hearted ‘idealist,’ a voleano in totally inef- 
fective eruption, a mover in movements, an utterer 
of leaflets, a compiler of ‘free literature.’ It seems 
remote enough now. Still it has left me a dyed-in- 
the-wool pacifist with a taste for Wellsian discus- 
sions, an intensified love of personal liberty and a 
strong dislike of the personified state. How much 
nicer Americans are than America! Columbia (hail), 
Britannia (rules the waves), Deutschland (iiber 
Alles), Sverige (fosterland), Erin (my grief) and so 
forth, are all very well in their way — like the 
League of Nations and the Brotherhood of Man. 
But I find as I grow older that when it comes down 
to brass tacks there is a deal more comfort in indi- 
vidual people and places than in vague abstrac- 
tions. I much prefer Louis Wilkinson to Czecho- 


Q72 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


Slovakia, I care more for bits of Surrey, Saint 
John’s Wood, Charlotte Street, Port-Cros, Malin, 
Ted, Tommy, Mahony and Martigues than for the 
Balkan League, the Little Entente or the safety of 
Armenia. Deeply as I love my own country and more 
deeply as I love my own village — London, Eng. 
— I demand the right to add as I please to my af- 
fections for places and for people. The war has left 
me, as it has left so many others of my generation, 
with a fixed decision never again to be stampeded 
by popular clamour into loves or hates. No more 
‘gallant allies,’ no more ‘cowardly enemies’! As 
Ezra Pound says in one of his poems, if I don’t mis- 
quote him, ‘I am homesick after mine own kind.’ | 
Some of ‘mine own kind’ I have found in every 
country I have ever visited: and not all the wars 
nor all the ‘propaganda’ in the world will ever 
- again force me, a free-born man, to modify my 
natural likes and dislikes by a hair’s-breadth. 
After all, there may have been something in Lillian’s 
contention when, in the New York speak-easy, she 
suddenly labelled me an ‘internationalist.’ Per- 
haps Iam. For wherever I find people who, as the 
saying is, “speak the same language,’ I never feel a 
foreigner. 


II 

On the following morning young B. and his wife, 
to whose care L. had entrusted me, called for me at 
my hotel and drove me to Plymouth and then on to 
the B. cranberry bogs. It was a cold, bright day, 
very tonic and bracing, and the drive in the open 
car did me all the good in the world. The country- 
side near Boston reminded me greatly of Sweden. 


BOSTON | 273 


There was something hard and cruel about it, a lack 
of natural fertility which must have tried the cour- 
age and tested the endurance of the puritan settlers. 
The wooden houses, some of them more than two 
centuries old, save for the fact that they were 
painted white instead of red or yellow, had also a 
very Swedish appearance. The fact that no railing, 
wall or fence separated one house and garden from 
another rather shocked my English notions of pri- 
vacy. I confess I like a garden wall and a garden 
gate! Plymouth, with the famous Rock, was rather 
a disappointment to me. Like so many Swedish 
country towns, it was clean and tidy but lacking in 
interest or ‘atmosphere.’ As we drove on, through 
a country of lakes and stunted forests, the resemb- 
lance to Sweden became more and more marked. 
The home of Mr. and Mrs. B., young B.’s parents, 
with its lovely situation overlooking a lake and a 
wide vista of forest, and its extreme loneliness and 
remoteness, reminded me very much of certain 
Swedish country-houses in the neighbourhood of 
Gothenburg. I have never been in the Swedish 
province of Norrland, where the Swedish cran- 
berries come from —lingon, cranberry jam, ap- 
pears on every Swedish dinner-table and is a 
national necessity — but I imagine it must be a 
country of bogs and lakes much resembling Mr. 
B.’s estate. 

After lunch, we drove to Cambridge, the suburb 
of Boston in which Harvard is situated. On our 
way I was shown the house in which Longfellow 
lived. It was just the kind of house in which he 
would have lived. As I looked at it I became aware 
of subtle emanations of New England uplift, and 


QA THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


the ghosts of Ralph Waldo Emerson, James Russell 
Lowell, and other nineteenth-century worthies 
seemed to lend a more than North Oxford smugness 
to these comparatively calm suburban streets. I 
was reminded by them a little of Tulse Hill, Streat- 
ham and Clapham, those homes not of the New 
England but of the South London ‘conscience.’ 


IV 
LITERARY EVENING 


I wap not been more than a week in New York 
when I was one day summoned to the telephone, 
and heard a rather drawling voice that seemed 
familiar and was certainly English. ‘Is that you, 
Goldring? Why didn’t you tell me before that you 
were in New York? Look here, won’t you come and 
dine with me to-night at the Arts’ Club...’ The 
voice was Ford Madox Ford’s. I was next door to 
the Arts’ Club when I received this message, in the 
hospitable ‘Players,’ for which I had been put up 
on arrival by a friendly publisher, and I was for- 
tunately free. 

The first thing I noticed about Ford, after we had 
greeted one another, was the fact that he seemed as 
completely at home in New York as he is in Mont- 
parnasse. The club was very full of people of both 
sexes, women perhaps predominating, and the arts 
were much in evidence. Pictures of all kinds cov- 
ered the walls, and in two large rooms on the ground 
floor were displayed all the season’s publications — 
a really admirable idea, this. We browsed among 
the new books for a few minutes, and then went in 
to dinner. No sooner had I opened my napkin than 
a strange thing happened. From the rather unduly 
talkative man of forty which I know myself to be, 
I found myself suddenly changing, slipping back 
into my own past, becoming once again a shy and 
tongue-tied youth, ‘having dinner with the editor.’ 
Filled with amused astonishment, I devoured my 


276 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


blue-fish, failed to eat my first ‘alligator pear’ (be- 
cause there was none left), and to the accompani- 
ment of Ford’s agreeable monotone sent my 
thoughts travelling back to the far-off days when 
he actually was my Olympian chief. And after 
dinner, while Ford was writing letters and I was 
alone with my cigarette, all sorts of memories came 
crowding in upon me. 

When I was a boy of twenty-one, just beginning 
to earn my living on the staff of ‘Country Life,’ 
after a few terms at Oxford, my Editor introduced 
me one morning to Mr. Ford Madox Hueffer. Ford 
at that time — it was I think the summer of 1908 — 
was already making his preparations for launching 
the famous first number of ‘The English Review’ 
and, much to my delight, he appointed me his sub- 
editor. I had at that period — and, despite appear- 
ances to the contrary, have never entirely lost it 
—a large amount of respect for the real heads of 
my profession, who, I need hardly say, are not neces- 
sarily those who make the most money or receive 
the largest amount of ‘publicity.’ Ford then, in 
those far-away days, struck me as one of the most 
awe-inspiring figures I had ever met; more awe- 
inspiring even than the captain of the First Eleven 
had been, when I was a new boy at school. 

My first spell of duty for ‘The English Review’ 
took the form of a week-end at his cottage in Kent. 
(As an employer Ford was always characteristically 
and incorrigibly hospitable.) The place was some- 
where on high ground, with a view across green 
marshlands to where Rye and Winchelsea stand 
dreaming by the sea. I seem to remember a long- 
ish, low house with various outbuildings, in one of 


LITERARY EVENING O77 


which I slept and in one of which I remember tak- 
ing down in longhand, at Ford’s dictation, one of 
the most graceful letters, to accompany a substan- 
tial cheque, which I imagine that any editor has 
ever written to a contributor, however distin- 
guished. The letter was to Thomas Hardy, to 
thank him for the poem called ‘A Sunday Morning 
Tragedy,’ which subsequently appeared at the be- 
ginning of the first number of the review. From it 
I got my first glimpse of how, in an ideal world, the 
old and great should be addressed by their younger 
confreres. 

The next incident which I can recall with any 
vividness was also a week-end, this time with Jo- 
seph Conrad, who was then living in a gloomy old 
farmhouse near Luton. I don’t exactly know why 
Ford took me down there with him, unless it was 
that in those days he was amusingly ‘baronial’ and 
regarded me as his ‘suite’ or retinue. Anyway, edi- 
tor and ‘retinue’ arrived at Luton on a dripping- 
wet afternoon, and got into a stuffy fly which very 
slowly splashed its way along the country lanes to 
Conrad’s door. The house was surrounded by trees 
and seemed to me unutterably melancholy. After 
dinner, Ford, Conrad, and the ‘retinue’ went up to 
Conrad’s big candle-lit study on the first floor, and 
there my elders talked while I sat in a corner and 
listened. And how they talked! Tirelessly, end- 
lessly, and in a mixture of French and English, they 
discussed the job of writing in general, and the 
composition of the first number of the review in 
particular. They discussed the technique of the 
novel and the short story, and quoted long passages 
from ‘Madame Bovary’ and ‘Un Coeur Simple’ at 


278 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


one another, with ever-growing excitement. I can 
see before me now Conrad’s short, broad-shouldered, 
long-armed figure and his dark, pointed beard and 
dark eyes, as he stood in the candlelight in front of 
the fire, with Ford, tall, blond and blue-eyed, by 
his side; and I can hear again Ford’s light-pitched, 
drawling tones mingling with Conrad’s deeper, 
more staccato notes. What I chiefly remember 
about the conversation, apart from the references 
to Flaubert and to the contents of the first number 
of the review, is that it hinged on the necessity for 
English writers to follow the example of the French 
and learn how they got their own effects. Too many 
even of our best writers, the inference was, have 
been artists by accident as it were, instead of as a 
result of deliberately disciplining their talents and 
learning ‘how.’ And I got an inkling, during that 
endless discussion, which, prolonged itself far into 
the night, of just what the review was intended to 
accomplish for English letters. It was going to set 
up and maintain a standard of literary values, of 
real writing. And in the first gallant year of its 
existence, despite incredible difficulties and un- 
imaginable turmoils of which at the time I knew 
little, no one can now say that it failed in its pur- 
pose. 

Ford, it must be admitted, made an original and 
slightly eccentric editor, nor could it well have 
been otherwise. One single individual could hardly 
be expected to combine a pure love of good letters, 
unusual critical insight, admirable gifts as poet and 
novelist and the most incorrigible sociability with 
those hard-faced commercial and administrative 
talents which produce the ‘paying concern.’ A 


LITERARY EVENING 279 


“paying concern’ ‘The English Review,’ during the 
year of Ford’s editorship, certainly was not. It was 
a gallant adventure nevertheless, and has well 
earned its place in our literary history. Difficulties 
and turmoils there were in plenty, during the agi- 
tated twelve months in which Ford occupied the 
editorial chair, though I was far too excited, far 
too youthfully self-centred and too inexperienced to 
appreciate them. For me that year was one long 
thrill: in a literary sense it was the real thing, at 
last. The little group of Oxford poets I had met in 
my undergraduate days and after — James Elroy 
Flecker, J. D. Beazley, Trelawney Dayrell-Reed 
and several others —had cast a glamour for me 
over the profession of letters. We all turned out 
verses, some of us with great facility, and dreamed 
of dying young and famous, though Flecker was the 
only one of our little group who was destined to 
turn this dream into a reality. But we were all 
contemporaries and beginners; and even for Flecker 
the magic of print was at that time hard of achieve- 
ment. Ford’s flat at 84 Holland Park Gardens, 
where the review was edited, was on the other hand 
filled daily, to overflowing, not with aspirants but 
with the tried, the tested, the acknowledged! The 
Beverly Nichols type of young man (which I am 
not yet too old to appreciate) scarcely existed 
twenty years ago. Perhaps if 1 had been endowed 
with some of the hard modern insouciance and dis- 
illusion I should have noticed in these, to me, au- 
gust figures only their queer clothes, their odd per- 
sonal habits and idiosyncrasies. As it was, they 
were the only sort of ‘great’ for which I had any 
respect. And I contrived to keep my two worlds 


280 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


entirely separate — the world in which I danced 
more or less efficiently and in which no one knew 
one end of a book from another, and the world in 
which I sat demurely in a corner and listened to my 
betters. Only once or twice did the water-tight 
compartments fail to act, and I shall never forget 
the shock I had at a dance at Kent House in 
Knightsbridge, when I heard my hostess ask Mr. 
Rudyard Kipling to take Mrs. Humphry Ward 
down to supper, and thus identified them. Nowa- 
days, I’m afraid, I take but little interest in en- 
countering the writers whose works I admire, in the 
flesh: and I have come to think the “public person- 
age’ about as cheerless as the public building. 
The manuscripts that were sent in to “The Eng- 
lish Review’ were housed in a very beautiful old 
inlaid Spanish cabinet, in Ford’s study. When I 
arrived in the evening — for I sub-edited ‘Country 
Life’ during the day, and usually did not appear at 
Holland Park Avenue before 6 p.m. — I was gener- 
ally despatched at once to the Shepherd’s Bush 
Empire, to secure a box or two stalls for the ‘second 
house.’ After dinner, we chartered a hansom and 
editor and ‘sub’ drove down to the music-hall, 
with the manuscripts which had been sent in during 
the day. During the performance, or rather during 
the duller turns, Ford made his decisions and I duly 
recorded them. But when somebody really worth 
listening to, the late Victoria Monks for example, 
appeared on the stage, the cares of editorship were 
for the moment laid aside. After the theatre we 
usually went back to the flat and worked on, some- 
times till 2 a.m. And that was, more or less, the 
way in which the most distinguished English liter- 


LITERARY EVENING 281 


ary periodical of the present century got itself into 
print. 

After all, there must have been a good deal to be 
said for the Shepherd’s Bush Empire, from Ford’s 
point of view. The atmosphere was conducive, 
there was no one to worry him and he could think 
undisturbed. In the flat, on the other hand, apart 
from the endless parties, stray callers seemed to 
arrive at all hours of the day or night, all of them, 
no doubt, bursting with advice, suggestions or com- 
plaints. By contrast, the music-hall must have 
seemed a haven of peace. 

At one of Ford’s tea-parties, I remember seeing a 
little quiet, grey old man who turned out to be 
Thomas Hardy. I was standing next to Hugh Wal- 
pole, at the back of the room, near the tea-table. 
The conversation among the lion-cubs in our neigh- 
bourhood was no doubt very brilliant and very 
‘literary, but suddenly there came the usual inex- 
plicable hush. It was broken by Hardy, who, turn- 
ing to an elderly lady by his side, remarked, ‘And 
how is Johnny’s whooping-cough?’ 

Conrad was, of course, frequently in the flat dur- 
ing the first months of our existence. J remember 
once being deputed to stay in after dinner and keep 
him company, until Ford returned from some party 
or other. Alas, on this occasion I missed an oppor- 
tunity. For, as it happened, I had an assignation 
that evening with a young person of the opposite 
sex, and my inability to keep it must so have pre- 
occupied my mind that I cannot now recall one 
word of a prolonged conversation. 

Of the young writers whom Ford, I will not say 
‘discovered’ — for that is a word which, in my ex- 


282 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


perience, invariably arouses rage — but whose gifts 
he was, at all events, quick to appreciate, the most 
outstanding figures were P. Wyndham Lewis and 
Ezra Pound. Both of them, at that period, in 
clothes, hairdressing and manner, made no secret of 
their calling. Pound contrived to look ‘every inch 
a poet’ (and still does), while I have never seen 
any one so obviously a ‘genius’ as Wyndham Lewis 
when, after his return from a sojourn abroad, he 
first appeared at Ford’s parties. 

The story (no doubt apocryphal) of Lewis’s first 
call at ‘The English Review’ office formed one of the 
many legends which collected round that singular 
flat. Lewis, tall, dark, and with romantically dis- 
ordered hair, wearing a long black garment but- 
toned up to his chin, arrived one morning at Num- 
ber 84 with the manuscript of ‘The Pole’ in his in- 
side coat-pocket. Getting no answer to his ring, he 
walked into the flat and searched the first-floor 
rooms, in vain, for an occupant. Undeterred, he 
climbed another flight of stairs and, hearing at last 
sounds of human life, he knocked at the door 
through which they came, and walked in. It hap- 
pened to be the bath-room, and there, reclining on 
his back in the bath, in two feet of hot water, with a 
large sponge in one hand and a piece of soap in the 
other, was the missing editor. Quite disregarding 
any unconventionality in his surroundings, Lewis at 
once proceeded to business. After announcing in 
the most matter-of-fact way that he was a man of 
genius and that he had a manuscript for publica- 
tion, he asked if he might read it. ‘Go on,’ said 
Ford, continuing to play with his sponge. Lewis 
then unbuttoned his coat, produced ‘The Pole’ and 


LITERARY EVENING 283 


read it aloud. At the end Ford observed, ‘ Well, 
that’s all right. If you leave it behind we'll cer- 
tainly print it.’ And the interview terminated. 
The literal truth of the whole or any part of the 
above story, of course, I cannot guarantee. All I 
can say is that if it didn’t happen, it ought to have 
happened. 

Other distinguished figures who came to the flat 
were R. B. Cunninghame Graham, who looked like 
a Spanish hidalgo (and wrote the most illegible 
hand it has ever been my lot to try to decipher), 
W. H. Hudson, Edward Garnett, Edward Thomas, 
Stephen Reynolds and the late Percival Gibbon. 
Gibbon was a merciless practical joker and used 
constantly to rag me about my efforts to get my 
poems published, once pretending that he had just 
heard that an eminent firm had decided to issue 
them in a limited large-paper edition. Alas, how 
eagerly did I allow myself to be deceived! On one 
occasion he played a joke on Ford with equal suc- 
cess. He arrived at the flat, late one snowy night, 
in a state of well-simulated horror and consterna- 
tion. ‘Ford, what d’you think has happened?’ he 
gasped. ‘I drove here in a four-wheeler, and just as 
we got near the Tube station the cab stopped sud- 
denly. I got out to see what was up, and found the 
poor old cabby dead on his box. We must go out and 
do something about it.” A wave of humanitarian 
emotion swept over Ford and his companion. 
Murmuring ‘doctor...brandy...cold and ex- 
posure... poor old fellow,’ they hurried into their 
coats and rushed forth into the icy street, followed 
by the chuckling Gibbon. 

Soon after Ford and ‘The English Review’ parted 


284 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


company, a small legacy came my way which, with 
the unwisdom of youth, I promptly ‘put into a 
paper’ and became, in my turn, an editor. It was 
an illustrated monthly magazine which quickly 
swallowed all the capital invested in it, and died, 
after a not altogether inglorious existence, within a 
year. Subsequently I became connected with a 
firm of publishers which issued at my instigation 
James Elroy Flecker’s “The Golden Journey to 
Samarkand,’ and also an edition of Ford’s ‘Col- 
lected Poems,’ which, out of natural metas, I pro- 
duced in as beautiful a form as I could contrive. It 
must now be a rare book, and I wish I possessed a 
copy of it myself. 

I was just wondering where the dickens my copy 
had got to when Ford finished his letters and sug- 
gested that we should go along to one of his ad- 
dresses — in New York, as in Paris, he defends his 
working-hours by having several retreats — where 
a party of young people were expecting us. It was 
November, but we were in the midst of a heat- 
wave and the night was warm and starry. The 
theatres and movies had already begun and the 
streets were pleasantly deserted, so that, as rarely 
happens in New York, we were able to stroll in 
comfort. As we passed into Washington Square, 
Ford pointed out to me the window of the room in 
which Henry James was born. And somehow, after 
that, it didn’t seem quite so singular that Ford and 
{ should be strolling about together in New York, 
after having, but a few months before, strolled 
about together, in Paris. For the great Henry was 
not the least august of the contributors to the re- 
view. Although I never met him, it fell to my lot to 


LITERARY EVENING 285 


read his proofs, and I remember once drawing 
Ford’s attention to a passage, in a story whose 
name I have forgotten, which struck me as odd. It 
was a question as to whether a gentleman wearing 
a top-hat could put his head, conveniently, through 
a carriage window and kiss its fair occupant. The 
point was submitted to the great man, who, I be- 
lieve, with plans and diagrams and the utmost elab- 
oration, explained precisely how the deed was done. 
When we arrived at our destination, it turned out 
to be a house in a street which Londoners would 
call “Early Victorian.’ It might easily have been a 
London street, for it had a name and not a number, 
and there were none of the usual New York fire- 
escapes disfiguring its facades. We entered by a 
basement door, which looked exactly like the en- 
trance to a ‘speak-easy,’ and making our way into 
a small back sitting-room, we found awaiting us 
round a deal table a troup of two or three young 
poets and a literary critic, not one of whom could 
have been more affluent than their opposite num- 
bers in Paris or in London. But it was easy to tell 
that they were all of them interested in writing for 
its own sake. Soon the conversation became criti- 
cal and technical — real literary talk, a little in the 
big bow-wow style — of a kind I hadn’t heard for 
nearly twenty years. For in England, somehow... 
I don’t know. Most of the young English writers I 
have come across have been elegant and moderately 
affluent personages who try to give the impression 
of stooping from Mayfair to conquer Bohemia. 
Their living is complicated rather than plain; and 
their ‘high-thinking’ is done ‘off stage.’ If they 
want to discuss literature, they either give public 


286 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


lectures or write essays or ‘middles.’ But here, in 
New York, was a group of young people who were 
talking quite easily and naturally about the subject 
which interested them most. Ford, of course, was 
very rightly encouraged to hold the floor. And for 
about an hour, with elbows on the table, he talked 
about books and their writers with a judgment, 
penetration, and wealth of knowledge, which few 
men in that city of lecturers could have equalled. I 
suppose he knew perfectly well that he was talking 
to men who took their jobs seriously, that the at- 
mosphere was right. And it was entirely in keeping 
with the Ford of ‘The English Review’ days that, 
with an engagement-book that could easily have 
been filled to overflowing with social functions he 
should elect to spend an evening at his ease, in a 
basement sitting-room, ina back street, talking about 
writing. That, to my mind, in an artist, is an indi- 
cation of purity; and of all the writers I have met 
Ford was, and is, the purest. Purity is a rare quality 
and a much-abused word. In the writer or the 
painter it seems to me to mean an undeviating, 
undistracted, continuous devotion to the art he 
practises, for its own sake. Nowadays, embittered 
perhaps by years and disillusion, I am no great re- 
specter of persons; and view most of the celebrities 
of my trade with suspicion and a certain cynicism. 
The thrusting arriviste who ‘gets there,’ whose 
‘prices’ are spoken of with bated breath and who is 
past-master in all the arts of publicity may, and 
often does, arouse admiration for his technical ac- 
complishment and his dexterity. But only the pure 
writer, to my mind, deserves respect — even if, as 
is sometimes the case, there is nothing respectable 


LITERARY EVENING 287 


about him. The ‘intention,’ as Catholics say, 
seems to have more to do with it even than achieve- 
ment. 

And so this was New York, the centre of dollar- 
chasing publicity-mongers, the big, crude, half- 
civilized city of the European imagination, the city 
of second-hand critical judgments borrowed from 
Europe, of boosting and graft and fake and sham! 
Something seemed to have gone wrong. Bang went 
another pre-conceived ‘impression’! For though, 
in England, instructed critical opinion has long 
given Ford his due, it has been reserved, so far, for 
America to provide in his lifetime that wide public 
for his works to which a writer of his eminence is 
obviously entitled. Honour, in short, where honour 
is due; and America must be honoured not least. 
Take it all round, of my all-too-few nights in New 
York, this one was the most unexpected and not 
the least delightful.

RETURNED EMPTY 
I 


‘THE whole ship was mine’ on the way home. She 
was a slow boat of about eleven thousand tons. 
Twenty-five years ago, under the Dutch flag, she 
had been considered quite a luxurious liner and had 
carried first and second class, as well as many steer- 
age passengers. Now she was given up to ‘tourist 
thirds’; and instead of the four or five hundred 
whom she could easily accommodate, she cleared 
from New York with no more than twenty passen- 
gers on board of her. It was exciting to watch the 
famous French and English liners, all blazing with 
lights and throbbing with life and movement, get- 
ting off ahead of us. Our forlorn little ship was the 
last to leave, and as we cast free from our moorings 
there was no one to cheer our departure or to wave 
a valedictory handkerchief. 

At breakfast the next morning the handful of 
passengers assembled, inspected each other, and re- 
lapsed into a sullen despair. Everybody on board 
was bored from the start, from the Captain to the 
members of the orchestra (who scraped out selec- 
tions from H.M.S. Pinafore at tea time, to an audi- 
ence of three); from the Doctor, who had no pa- 
tients, to the barman who sold no drinks. The At- 
lantic Ocean, to which I had already taken a strong 
dislike, soon revealed itself as the most insufferably 
tedious expanse of dirty grey liquid it has ever been 
my lot to float on. It wasn’t even rough. A flirta- 


RETURNED EMPTY 289 


tion of the kind one reads about in the popular 
novels, would have been a God-send. ‘Booze- 
hounds,’ had there been any on the ship, would 
have found little difficulty in weaning me from my 
accustomed abstemiousness. But the bar was as 
cheerless as a disused Baptist Chapel. 

One couldn’t even pass away the hours by swap- 
ping lies and re-roasting stale chestnuts. The library 
was composed only of books I did not want to read; 
and of my own novels the only one it possessed was 
a work which I would not now willingly inflict even 
on my worst enemy. Three or four of my novels, 
especially after a good dinner, I confess that I can 
re-read with interest, wondering how the dickens I 
could have been so clever five, ten even fifteen years 
ago. But to this one, no, not even the best of din- 
ners would ever reconcile me. The few well-mean- 
ing souls who took it out of the bookcase returned 
it hastily. They had all my sympathy. 

There was just simply nothing to be done on this 
dismal voyage home except to review the past and 
peer anxiously into the future. Through no fault of 
my own, every imaginable thing about my Ameri- 
can trip had gone awry. My affairs, from first to 
last, had been mishandled for me, and now here I 
was being hauled back across the ocean just at the 
moment when I was beginning to find my feet in 
the New World. The delays of my London pub- 
lisher had caused the postponment of the New York 
edition of my novel ‘Facade’ till the spring, so that 
I missed even the pleasure (or the mortification) of 
reading my American reviews. How many other 
more important things had I not missed? My last 
hurried days in New York were spent in declining 


290 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


invitations which I should have loved to have been 
able to accept. Indeed, hardly any of the things 
which I had determined to do and see, during my 
visit to America, had I seen or done. Fate had de- 
creed that I should be ‘returned empty,’ devoid 
alike of dollars or of any specifically American ex- 
periences. 

On our third day out I was joined in my daily 
perambulations by a young American engineer 
of central European extraction. He was short, 
swarthy and rather overpowering and aggressive in 
manner. In a way he was the modern post-war 
American imitation of the ‘superior’ Englishman 
of the days of England’s unchallenged supremacy. 
He was a characteristic specimen of that type of 
American which, much to our chagrin, has wrested 
from the English the proud distinction of being the 
world’s most hated race. He and his kind, descend- 
ing as conquerors upon stricken Europe — which, 
by the way, is beginning, despite the pessimists, 
once again to sit up and take nourishment — have 
managed positively to endear John Bull to the 
Continental heart. He always called the French 
the ‘poor frogs,’ and was at pains to tell me that 
‘A.E.F.’ meant ‘After England Failed.’ His genial 
contempt for my own worn-out, senile, ‘hard- 
boiled’ country cheered me up considerably. One 
day, when I was talking to an Englishwoman about 
home and referring to places that we both knew 
and loved, our American friend butted in with some 
information. ‘Oh, England,’ he said with crushing 
finality, ‘the country’s nice enough, but the towns 
are ahful.’ My countrywoman and I relapsed into 
silence, after this, both thinking of an English town 


RETURNED EMPTY 291 


or two of our acquaintance for which even an 
American Jew might have shown some respect. 
But it was impossible to be angry with him: he radi- 
ated geniality and kindliness of the sort that grates 
on every sensitive European nerve. During several 
weeks in New York I had never once met anybody 
in the least like him. In European travel, on the 
other hand, as regards casual acquaintances, I 
rarely come across any other type of American. 
Why is it, I wonder? He was just the sort of man 
who, if he managed to produce a play in London 
with success, would cable to his agent to try to get 
some member of our Royal family to make a state- 
ment about it for publicity purposes. I gathered 
that if any eminent person was pointed out to him 
in the streets of a European city, he went right up 
and slapped him on the back, introduced himself, 
talked politics, explained the American viewpoint. 
As we drew daily nearer Boulogne, his pride and 
confidence increased. It was clear that for him the 
‘beaten’ countries were France with her cheap 
franc, and England with her colossal burden of her 
own and other nations’ debts. There is, of course, 
something to be said for this idea; and the position 
of Germany to-day lends colour to the argument. 
Englishmen and Frenchmen, however, do not take 
kindly to the notion that their gallant American 
allies joined in the war on their side merely to lay 
them out and take their money. Nor do they be- 
lieve it to be true. I tried tactfully to indicate how 
both the ‘ poor frogs’ and the senile English regarded 
the matter, and in a moment his face became all 
over one great beam of geniality and friendliness. 
It was like a sunburst. He smacked me on the back, 


292 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


said, ‘Boy, put it right there,’ gripped my hand, 
asserted that ‘we’ — ‘frogs’ and Englishmen in- 
cluded — were just about the grandest bunch of 
fellows, if the truth were known, that ever got to- 
gether, and tried to drag me forcibly to the cocktail 
bar. Well, I ask you? 


II 


We reached Boulogne at last, and were put on 
shore in the early hours of a foggy morning, in time 
to get a few hours’ sleep before the Paris rapide bore 
us off to America’s European headquarters. The 
tasks which awaited me on arrival in France were 
of such a nature that it was many months before I 
was able to give any sort of connected thought to 
my New York experiences. But, when at last there 
came a respite from distresses, and I was able to 
bask in the healing sunshine of Juan-les-Pins, a 
well-earned idleness and warmth induced a mood 
of agreeable reflection. Two old friends, one of 
whom had ten years’ experience of lecturing in 
America, were my guests and the conversation 
naturally turned often enough on the Great Re- 
public. While we sat after bathing, in red cane 
armchairs on the agreeable terrasse of the ‘Poti- 
niére,’ which happened to be next door to our flat, 
we sipped the bronxes brought to us by the smiling 
Francois, and exchanged impressions. We talked 
about the glamour and excitement of New York, its 
breakneck pace, and of the contrast between its 
strange, lovely, rather terrifying sky-scrapers and 
its slipshod untidiness and squalor, its peculiar lack 
of style, its noise and its traffic jams, and the ro- 
mance which enveloped it. ‘How should it not be 


RETURNED EMPTY 293 


untidy,’ said M., ‘when the average life of a building 
in New York is about a generation? What is the 
use of planting gardens in Washington Square when 
in a year or two the builders will be feverishly 
transforming it? Why, even Gramercy Park, which 
until quite recently was a little oasis of mellowed 
beauty and calm, has now been half rebuilt. In 
New York nothing is permanent except the rock of 
Manhattan and the great rivers which flow past it. 
No wonder the New Yorkers are restless, no wonder 
the tempo of New York life is feverish in its rapid- 
ity! No wonder, also, that Europe both attracts and 
exasperates Americans, and that they both attract 
and exasperate Europeans.’ 

“Why should Europe exasperate them?’ I asked. 
“They seem to love it.’ 

‘It exasperates them,’ said M., ‘because all the 
things which are most highly valued and respected 
in European countries are things which money can- 
not buy. It’s a difference in values. Americans 
here are still constantly coming up against doors 
which all the gold of Golconda cannot open. The 
greatest danger for the world that I can foresee is 
that European countries, England especially, should 
become entirely Americanized. If we don’t wake up 
pretty soon and put a stop to it, the damage will be 
done and the whole world will be the poorer — 
America included. America especially. There is a 
certain symbolic significance in the old chestnut 
about the American tourist who was shown, in an 
Italian shrine, a sanctuary lamp which had been 
kept continuously burning, by the faithful, for 
seven hundred years. “I guess it’s out now,” he 
remarked, as he spat and extinguished it. Europe 


294 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


still keeps alight the sacred lamps of one kind of 
civilization. America, not content with developing 
her own brand, cannot help, half-unconsciously, 
trying to destroy ours. If Asia is the cradle of all 
religions, Europe is the continent which has done 
most to prepare the ordinary “man of action”’ for 
the acceptation of religion by developing scholar- 
ship, the arts, personal honour, and “principle.” 
**Principle”’ includes all intellectual integrity, such 
as that of Socrates or Giordano Bruno; all patriot- 
ism worthy of the name; all “causes”’ for which 
men have justifiably sacrificed their lives; all “ pub- 
lic spirit.”’ It is a first step on the ladder of spiritual 
progress. Principle begins at the point where it is 
able to defeat self-interest. The man of principle is 
the unbuyable man. However humble, he can make 
all the Rockefellers, Monds, Morgans, and Mellons 
look foolish. Such men have always existed in 
Europe since classical times and our most unprin- 
cipled blackguards are at least nurtured in respect 
for them. “Principle,” in short, is one of our “‘sanc- 
tuary lamps.” 

‘Of course America also abounds with “men of 
principle.”’ The Sacco and Vanzetti case showed 
that. But it still more clearly showed the astonish- 
ing inability of the majority of Americans even to 
understand the meaning of the word. The out- 
burst of rage throughout the world, from China to 
Peru, which the executions aroused, took most of 
them entirely by surprise. “What’s biting you 
people?” a friend asked me, at the time. “They 
were only a couple of wops, anyway.” What we 
call petty theft, Americans call “‘collecting souve- 
nirs’’; and they look on card games quite inno- 


RETURNED EMPTY 295 


cently, as a duel of wits to see who wins. It’s per- 
fectly fair if you know the ropes. On the other 
hand, if most Americans have substituted emotions 
for principles, they are as a rule boundlessly idealis- 
tic and really warm-hearted. And the American 
idealist is, let us admit it, the salt of the earth. But 
on the whole, despite the value for the world in gen- 
eral.of its fresh idealism, the mechanized civiliza- 
tion of America is best suited to the country which 
has evolved it. We, in Europe, can learn much 
from it and can assimilate much; but not too much, 
otherwise our own civilization will wither. During 
the war, scared leader-writers used to tell us how 
terrible it would be if Germany won it. Actually if 
Germany, by defeating us, had given an impetus to 
English music, inspired us to establish State-sup- 
ported opera, taught us how to make artistically 
satisfying films and how to reorganize our vital 
industries, we should have had something to con- 
sole us for our wounded pride. The American 
intervention in the war, which saved us from that 
once-dreaded result, a “‘stalemate,”’ has brought us 
none of these advantages. We shall have to pay her, 
for three generations, a colossal tribute; and, far 
from gaining any cultural benefits from her, she is 
vulgarizing great masses of our population by the 
flood of bad films she sends to us from Hollywood 
and is carrying on an unconcealed Pro-American 
propaganda throughout our Empire by the same 
means. Her war wealth enables her to buy up our 
art treasures from their impoverished owners; and 
in exchange she sends us jazz and inoculates our 
business men with “ideals of service’’ —the worst 
ideals she has. For some unknown reason England 


296 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


has gone America-mad. Her prevalent lack of 
principle in regard to serious social contracts, like 
marriage, is being eagerly imitated in England. 
Even her architecture, which, though admirable in 
America, is totally unsuited to our conditions, is 
being imitated, while the digestions of our young 
people are being ruined through their adopting the 
American habit of consuming short, sharp, and 
violent alcoholic drinks before meals. 

‘All this,’ said M., beginning now slightly to 
boom, ‘since America did not defeat us in the war, 
but only got the better of us, seems to me unneces- 
sary. We cannot blame the Americans for it; we 
can only blame ourselves. We should be all the bet- 
ter friends with America if we could only stop imi- 
tating her. Friends we are, thank God, and friends 
we shall remain. Anything like a war between 
America and Europe would lead inevitably to the 
destruction of both our civilizations, and to the 
domination of the World by Asia — under Russian 
leadership — and Africa. America is not only genu- 
inely pacifist, but intelligent enough to realize that 
wars don’t pay the countries that begin them.’ 

M. at this juncture so far forgot his prejudice 
against short, sharp, and violently alcoholic drinks 
before meals, as to consent to imbibe another bronx. 
Wellsian speculations are thirst-provoking; and 
M.’s picture of bolshevized Asiatics laying the 
brown man’s burden on the white man’s shoulders, 
in the event of Armageddon, seemed to justify a 
sudden stimulant. 

A young American couple, who love London as 
much as we do and have made their home in it, 
came in to the Potiniére, while we were recovering, 


RETURNED EMPTY 297 


and sat at our table. ‘Bow-wow’ themes collapsed 
immediately. Such dears! And just as hard up as 
ourselves. Europe is indeed overrun with delightful 
Americans, cultivated and charming, with impec- 
cable manners and faultless taste, who are entirely 
without visible means of subsistence. Like the 
Russian aristocrats they keep going, somehow. But 
how? On the other hand our big hotels, our famous 
restaurants, haunts of established splendour, are 
painfully full of strident trans-Atlantics, gorged 
with money, who, to judge from their demeanour, 
might twenty years ago have been heaving coal or 
handing things on trays. But I, for one, shall not 
join in any depreciation of the nouveau-riche. Bet- 
ter the parvenu than the déclassé any day of the 
week, especially on the Céte d’Azur! 

While we were talking to Jack and Virginia, the 
folly of all generalizations about peoples and coun- 
tries, especially of generalizations about America, 
came over me once more, for the hundredth time. 
M. with all his eloquence, had, like Mr. Wells’ 
American, just been going ‘orn and orn and orn.’ 
Jack’s presence reminded me of one significant fact 
about our two countries, which seemed to me of 
more importance than a library full of weighty 
pronouncements. Among foreign countries, in 
which one does the average Englishman have the 
largest number of personal friends? Undoubtedly 
in the United States. And among European na- 
tions, with which country have travelled Americans 
the closest ties through personal friendships and 
intermarriage? Surely with England. 

Virginia restored our tone by telling us a highly 
amusing and not too proper story and Jack con- 


298 THE TRIP TO THE STATES 


vulsed us by imitating to the life our pseudo- 
military béte noire. Even M. himself, prophetic and 
reverberant, ceased to ponder World Problems. 
Instead, he invited us all to luncheon on the terrace 
of the Casino, where subsequently, under the mel- 
lowing influence of lobster mayonnaise and Mon- 
trachet, the process of getting together was satis- 
factorily accomplished. A kindly twinkle came into 
the eye of Columbia’s eagle, and John Bull’s lion, 
comatose and contented, wagged a friendly tail. 
The sun shone down on the lazy blue sea, and on 
the striped awnings, and on the bronzed backs of 
the bathers, while we, sitting round our shadowed 
table, decided that the world was after all far too 
good a place for the quarrelsome to be allowed to 
spoil it.
